Simultaneous linear symplectic reduction and orbit fibrations
Hyunmoon Kim

Abstract
We develop a correspondence between the orbits of the group of linear symplectomorphisms of a real finite dimensional symplectic vector space in the complex Lagrangian Grassmannian and the Grassmannians of linear subspaces of the real symplectic vector space. Under this correspondence, orbit fibration maps whose fibers are holomorphic arc components correspond to fibrations from simultaneous linear symplectic reduction. We use this to compute the homotopy types of Grassmannians of linear subspaces of the symplectic vector space in the general case, recovering the observations of Arnold in the Lagrangian case, Oh-Park in the coisotropic case, and Lee-Leung in the symplectic case. Binary octahedral symmetries, symplectic twistor Grassmannians, and symmetries of Jacobi forms appear within this structure.



1
Introduction

This paper achieves three main results.

First, we compute the homotopy type of the Grassmannian of linear subspaces of a real, finite dimensional, symplectic vector space in the general case. In the Lagrangian, (co)isotropic, and symplectic cases, we recover the results of
[
Arn67
]
,
[
OP05
]
,
[
LL11
]
,
[
AB13
]
. Simultaneous linear symplectic reduction naturally realizes these Grassmannians as total spaces of fiber bundles, whose base space is an isotropic Grassmannian and whose fiber space is a symplectic Grassmannian.

Second, we prepare the orbit fibrations of
[
Tak68
]
,
[
Wol72
]
,
[
Wol69
]
for applications to symplectic geometry. We spend some time to reformulate their results in the specific case of the complex Lagrangian Grassmannian, in order to expose the property that the orbit fibration maps
do not
depend on the choice of basepoint of the orbits, but
do
depend on the choice of compatible linear complex structure on the real symplectic vector space.

Third, we remove the mystery in the correspondence observed in
[
Kim22
]
. In
[
Kim22
]
, it was observed that there is a bijection between the Grassmannians of linear subspaces of a real, finite dimensional symplectic vector space, and the orbits of the group of linear symplectomorphisms of the symplectic vector space, such that the corresponding objects are homotopy equivalent (See Figure
A.3
for an example). We refine this correspondence to a correspondence of fiber bundles.

We now describe the structure of Grassmannians of linear subspaces of a real symplectic vector space.

The linear subspaces of a symplectic vector space interact with the symplectic form in different ways. For instance, if
W
is a linear subspace of a
2n
-dimensional real symplectic vector space
(V,\omega)
, its symplectic complement

W^{\omega}:=\{\mathbf{v}\in V:\omega(\mathbf{v},\mathbf{w})=0,\text{for all }\mathbf{w}\in W\}

may intersect itself in any dimension from
0
to
\dim_{\mathbb{R}}W
.

It is convenient to label linear subspaces
W\subseteq V
by
types
, a partition of
n
into a triple of nonnegative integers
\vec{n}=(n_{0},n_{+},n_{-})
, where
n_{0}=\dim_{\mathbb{R}}(W\cap W^{\omega})
,
\dim_{\mathbb{R}}(W/(W\cap W^{\omega}))=2n_{+}
, and
\dim_{\mathbb{R}}W^{\omega}/(W\cap W^{\omega})=2n_{-}
(in the spirit of Definition 3.1 of
[
Hes06
]
). The Grassmannians of linear subspaces of
V
of type
\vec{n}
are denoted by
\operatorname{Gr}(\vec{n})=\operatorname{Gr}(\vec{n};V)
. The Grassmannians
\operatorname{Gr}(\vec{n})
are the orbits of the real linear symplectic group

G:=\operatorname{Sp}(V)

in the ordinary real Grassmannian manifolds of
V
(Figure
A.1
). The common assumptions
W=W^{\omega}
(Lagrangian),
W^{\omega}\subseteq W
(coisotropic),
W\subseteq W^{\omega}
(isotropic), and
W\cap W^{\omega}=\{0\}
(symplectic), are equivalent to the type label satisfying, respectively
n_{0}=n
,
n_{-}=0
,
n_{+}=0
, and
n_{0}=0
. In addition, the type label provides a way to also systematically collect linear subspaces of
V
that are neither symplectic nor isotropic nor coisotropic.

Because linear subspaces interact with the symplectic form in different ways, the Grassmannians
\operatorname{Gr}(\vec{n})
exhibit different topological properties. For instance, some Grassmannians
\operatorname{Gr}(\vec{n})
are compact and others are not (e.g. Corollary
2.5
), and some are simply connected and others are not (e.g. (
72
)). This differs from the behavior of ordinary real or complex Grassmannians.

The Grassmannians of linear subspaces of real symplectic vector spaces have been studied type by type. In Lemma 1.2 of
[
Arn67
]
, it was shown that the Lagrangian Grassmannian
\operatorname{Gr}(n,0,0)
is diffeomorphic to
\operatorname{U}(n)/O(n)
. In Proposition 2.1 of
[
OP05
]
, it was shown that the coisotropic Grassmannian
\operatorname{Gr}(n_{0},n_{+},0)
is diffeomorphic to
\operatorname{U}(n)/(O(n_{0})\times\operatorname{U}(n_{+}))
. It was shown in Theorem 13 of
[
LL11
]
, and Theorem 1.1 of
[
AB13
]
in more generality, that the symplectic Grasmannian
\operatorname{Gr}(0,n_{+},n_{-})
(identified as a connected component of the oriented symplectic Grassmannian) strongly deformation retracts to the ordinary complex Grassmannian (or
\operatorname{U}(n_{+}+n_{-})/(\operatorname{U}(n_{+})\times\operatorname{U}(n_{-}))
). In the isotropic and coisotropic cases, one can choose a fixed
\omega
-compatible linear complex structure
\mathbf{J}
and work with basis extensions that are both
\omega(\cdot,\mathbf{J}\cdot)
-orthonormal and Darboux. In the symplectic and general case, imposing this convenient condition on the bases no longer sees all the linear subspaces, but does capture the entire homotopy type.

As a corollary to our main theorem, we compute the unsurprising homotopy type of the Grassmannians
\operatorname{Gr}(\vec{n})
in the general case.

Corollary

(Corollary
4.7
)
.

Let
(V,\omega)
be a
2n
-dimensional symplectic vector space and
\vec{n}=(n_{0},n_{+},n_{-})
be a triple of nonnegative integers that sum to
n
. Then the Grassmannian
\operatorname{Gr}(\vec{n};V)
is homotopy equivalent to
\operatorname{U}(n)/(O(n_{0})\times\operatorname{U}(n_{+})\times\operatorname{U}(n_{-}))
.

We now state our main theorem in a way that emphasizes the effect of making a choice of
\omega
-compatible linear complex structure
\mathbf{J}
:

Theorem

(Theorem
4.5
, Proposition
3.13
)
.

Let
(V,\omega)
be a real,
2n
-dimensional symplectic vector space, and
G=\operatorname{Sp}(V)
be the group of linear symplectomorphisms of
V
. Suppose
\mathbf{J}
is an
\omega
-compatible linear complex structure on
V
, and let
K=G^{\operatorname{Ad}(\mathbf{J})}
be the maximal compact subgroup of
G
, consisting of linear symplectomorphisms of
V
that commute with
\mathbf{J}
. Then there exists a compact submanifold (see Definition
3.12
or below)
\operatorname{Gr}_{\mathbf{J}}(\vec{n};V)\subseteq\operatorname{Gr}(\vec{n};V)
diffeomorphic to the left coset space

\operatorname{U}(n)/(O(n_{0})\times\operatorname{U}(n_{+})\times\operatorname{U}(n_{-})),

and a
K
-equivariant fiber bundle
\gamma_{\mathbf{J}}:\operatorname{Gr}(\vec{n};V)\to\operatorname{Gr}_{\mathbf{J}}(\vec{n};V)
such that each fiber has a complex structure induced by
\mathbf{J}
, and is biholomorphic to

\operatorname{U}(n_{+},n_{-})/(\operatorname{U}(n_{+})\times\operatorname{U}(n_{-})).

The fibers of
\gamma_{\mathbf{J}}
are biholomorphic to a Siegel domain of genus
1
in the sense of
[
Pya69
]
, and to the underlying complex manifold of a hermitian symmetric space of noncompact type AIII in the notation of
[
Hel01
]
. As topological spaces, the fibers are contractible, so by a result of
[
Dol63
]
, we obtain a strong deformation retract of the total space to the base space.

We now show how the Grassmannians
\operatorname{Gr}(\vec{n})
are also naturally total spaces of fiber bundles given by simultaneous linear symplectic reduction.

For any linear subspace
W\subseteq V
its linear symplectic reduction can be expressed as a short exact sequence of vector spaces

0\to W\cap W^{\omega}\to W\to W/(W\cap W^{\omega})\to 0.

If
W
has type
\vec{n}
,
W\cap W^{\omega}
is a type
(n_{0},0,n-n_{0})
subspace of
V
and
W/(W\cap W^{\omega})
is a type
(0,n_{+},n_{-})
subspace in
(W+W^{\omega})/(W\cap W^{\omega}))
. Since
W+W^{\omega}=(W\cap W^{\omega})^{\omega}
, we can consider all short exact sequences for all
W\subseteq V
of a given type simultaneously, and obtain a
G=\operatorname{Sp}(V)
-equivariant fiber bundle (Theorem
3.9
)

(\cdot\cap\cdot^{\omega}):\operatorname{Gr}(\vec{n};V)\to\operatorname{Gr}(n_{0},0,n-n_{0};V)

such that the fibers
(\cdot\cap\cdot^{\omega})^{-1}(W_{0})
are identified with
\operatorname{Gr}(0,n_{+},n_{-};W_{0}^{\omega}/W_{0})
.

Now let
\operatorname{Gr}_{\mathbf{J}}(\vec{n};V)
be the submanifold of
\operatorname{Gr}(\vec{n};V)
consisting of linear subspaces
W
of type
\vec{n}
such that
W\oplus\mathbf{J}(W\cap W^{\omega})
is
\mathbf{J}
-invariant. In the isotropic and coisotropic cases
\operatorname{Gr}_{\mathbf{J}}(\vec{n};V)
coincides with
\operatorname{Gr}(\vec{n};V)
, and in the symplectic case, the submanifolds
\operatorname{Gr}_{\mathbf{J}}(\vec{n};V)
coincide with ordinary complex Grassmannians (when
\operatorname{Gr}(\vec{n};V)
is identified with a connected component of the Grassmannian of oriented symplectic subspaces) of the complex vector space
(V,\mathbf{J})
. If
W\in\operatorname{Gr}_{\mathbf{J}}(\vec{n};V)
, then

(W\cap W^{\omega})^{\omega}/(W\cap W^{\omega})
has a reduced symplectic form
\tilde{\omega}
and
\mathbf{J}
prescribes a way to identify it with the
\omega(\cdot,\mathbf{J}\cdot)
-orthogonal complement of
(W\cap W^{\omega})\oplus\mathbf{J}(W\cap W^{\omega})
in
V
, which is a
\mathbf{J}
-invariant symplectic subspace of
V
. From this identification
(W\cap W^{\omega})^{\omega}/(W\cap W^{\omega})
obtains an
\tilde{\omega}
-compatible linear complex structure
\tilde{\mathbf{J}}
.

The
K
-equivariant (
\tilde{K}
-equivariant) fiber bundles
\gamma_{\mathbf{J}}
(
\gamma_{\tilde{\mathbf{J}}}
) occur in the following commuting diagram

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\operatorname{Gr}(0,n_{+},n_{-})}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\operatorname{Gr}(\vec{n})}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\operatorname{Gr}(n_{0},0,n-n_{0})}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\operatorname{Gr}_{\tilde{\mathbf{J}}}(0,n_{+},n_{-})}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\operatorname{Gr}_{\mathbf{J}}(\vec{n})}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\operatorname{Gr}_{\mathbf{J}}(n_{0},0,n-n_{0})}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\gamma_{\tilde{\mathbf{J}}}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\cdot\cap\cdot^{\omega}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\gamma_{\mathbf{J}}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\cdot\cap\cdot^{\omega}}

(1)

where the spaces in the bottom row are compact. After choosing a choice of Darboux basis of
V
compatible with a choice of basepoint from each space, the fiber bundle of the bottom row can be expressed as

\operatorname{U}(n)/(\operatorname{O}(n_{0})\times\operatorname{U}(n_{+})\times\operatorname{U}(n_{-}))\to\operatorname{U}(n-n_{+})/(\operatorname{O}(n_{0})\times\operatorname{U}(n_{-}))

with fibers diffeomorphic to
\operatorname{U}(n_{+}+n_{-})/(\operatorname{U}(n_{+})\times\operatorname{U}(n_{-}))
.

We now discuss our reformulation of a specific case of the results of Takeuchi
[
Tak68
]
and Wolf
[
Wol72
]
,
[
Wol69
]
.

A complex Lagrangian subspace
\mathbf{F}\subseteq V\otimes_{\mathbb{R}}\mathbb{C}
is a complex linear subspace that is maximally isotropic with respect to the
\mathbb{C}
-bilinear extension of
\omega
. Complex Lagrangian subspaces can also be labelled by type (Definition 3.1 of
[
Hes06
]
) where
(n_{0},n_{+},n_{-})
is the inertia of the hermitian form
-i\omega^{\mathbb{C}}(\cdot,\overline{\cdot})|_{\mathbf{F}}
. Complex Lagrangian subspaces with the same type
\vec{n}
can be collected into
\operatorname{Lag}^{\mathbb{C}}(\vec{n})=\operatorname{Lag}^{\mathbb{C}}(\vec{n};V)
and coincide with the
G=\operatorname{Sp}(V)
-orbits of the complex Lagrangian Grassmannian
\operatorname{Lag}^{\mathbb{C}}(V)
, the collection of all complex Lagrangian subspaces of
V\otimes_{\mathbb{R}}\mathbb{C}
.

We state the reformulation of the results of
[
Tak68
]
,
[
Wol72
]
,
[
Wol69
]
in a way that emphasizes both the dependence on the choice of
\omega
-compatible linear complex structure
\mathbf{J}
, and the structural similarity with our main theorem.

Theorem

(
[
Tak68
]
,
[
Wol72
]
,
[
Wol69
]
)
.

Let
(V,\omega)
be a real,
2n
-dimensional symplectic vector space, and
G=\operatorname{Sp}(V)
be the group of linear symplectomorphisms of
V
. Suppose
\mathbf{J}
is an
\omega
-compatible linear complex structure on
V
, and let
K
be the maximal compact subgroup of
G
consisting of linear symplectomorphisms of
V
that commute with
\mathbf{J}
. Then there exists a compact submanifold (see Definition
3.12
)
\operatorname{Lag}^{\mathbb{C}}_{\mathbf{J}}(\vec{n};V)\subseteq\operatorname{Lag}^{\mathbb{C}}(\vec{n};V)
diffeomorphic to the left coset space

\operatorname{U}(n)/(O(n_{0})\times\operatorname{U}(n_{+})\times\operatorname{U}(n_{-}))

and a
K
-equivariant fiber bundle
\beta_{\mathbf{J}}:\operatorname{Lag}^{\mathbb{C}}(\vec{n};V)\to\operatorname{Lag}_{\mathbf{J}}^{\mathbb{C}}(\vec{n};V)
such that each fiber has a complex structure induced by
\mathbf{J}
, and is biholomorphic to

(\operatorname{Sp}(2n_{+};\mathbb{R})/\operatorname{U}(n_{+}))\times(\operatorname{Sp}(2n_{-};\mathbb{R})/\operatorname{U}(n_{-})).

In
[
Tak68
]
,
[
Wol72
]
,
[
Wol69
]
, orbit fibrations were investigated in the generality of orbits of real semisimple Lie groups in irreducible hermitian symmetric spaces of compact type and complex flag manifolds. The complex Lagrangian Grassmannian occurs as a special case (Section 11 of
[
Wol72
]
). However, the homotopy type of
\operatorname{Lag}_{\mathbf{J}}^{\mathbb{C}}(\vec{n};V)
was only fully identified in the symplectic case, and the Grassmannians
\operatorname{Gr}(\vec{n})
occur as
G=\operatorname{Sp}(V)
-orbits of ordinary real Grassmannians, so they were also not discussed in
[
Tak68
]
,
[
Wol72
]
,
[
Wol69
]
. In addition to exposing the role of choosing an
\omega
-compatible linear complex structure
\mathbf{J}
, we contribute the identification of the homotopy type in the general case, and an application of their methods to smooth manifolds that are not necessarily complex flag manifolds.

The fibers of
\beta_{\mathbf{J}}
are biholomorphic to a product of two Siegel domains of genus
1
in the sense of
[
Pya69
]
, and underlying complex manifolds of a product of two hermitian symmetric spaces of noncompact type CI in the notation of
[
Hel01
]
. As topological spaces, they are contractible, so again by
[
Dol63
]
, we obtain a strong deformation retract of the total space to the base space, also obtaining a computation of the homotopy type of each orbit.

The construction of
\beta_{\mathbf{J}}
in the context of the diagram

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\operatorname{Lag}^{\mathbb{C}}(0,n_{+},n_{-})}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\operatorname{Lag}^{\mathbb{C}}(\vec{n})}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\operatorname{Gr}(n_{0},0,n-n_{0})}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\operatorname{Lag}_{\tilde{\mathbf{J}}}^{\mathbb{C}}(0,n_{+},n_{-})}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\operatorname{Lag}_{\mathbf{J}}^{\mathbb{C}}(\vec{n})}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\operatorname{Gr}_{\mathbf{J}}(n_{0},0,n-n_{0})}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\beta_{\tilde{\mathbf{J}}}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\operatorname{Re}\cdot_{0}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\beta_{\mathbf{J}}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\operatorname{Re}\cdot_{0}}

(2)

was stated in Theorem B of
[
Tak68
]
for the symplectic case and in Theorem 11.8 of
[
Wol69
]
for the general case. The map
\operatorname{Re}\cdot_{0}
appears in Theorem A.3 of
[
Tak68
]
, and Theorem 8.15 of
[
Wol69
]
. In Theorem 8.15.1b and Theorem 10.9.3 of
[
Wol69
]
the fibers of
\operatorname{Re}\cdot_{0}
are identified as
holomorphic arc components
of
\operatorname{Lag}^{\mathbb{C}}(V)
(One implication of this result is that for two points in different fibers of
\operatorname{Re}\cdot_{0}
, there does not exist a holomorphic open disk passing through both of them).

Our symplectic approach differs from
[
Tak68
]
,
[
Wol72
]
,
[
Wol69
]
in the following ways. First, our approach is more conveniently applicable to symplectic geometry because all constructions are made from
(V,\omega)
, we do not fix a root system of the Lie algebra of
G=\operatorname{Sp}(V)
, and we allow the complex structure on the complex Lagrangian Grassmannian to explicitly vary according to the choice of
\omega
-compatible linear complex structure
\mathbf{J}
on
(V,\omega)
. We provide a dictionary in Section
A.4
. Second, simultaneous linear symplectic reduction is manifestly
G
-equivariant, and we intentionally state it as a
G
-equivariant map. This differs from
[
Wol72
]
and
[
Wol69
]
, where in Theorem 8.15 of
[
Wol69
]
,
\operatorname{Re}\cdot_{0}
is explicitly
G
-equivariant, but in Section 11 of
[
Wol69
]
and the corollary to the holomorphic arc component theorem of
[
Wol72
]
, it is intentionally restated as a
K
-equivariant map. Third, we emphasize that
\beta_{\mathbf{J}}
s and
\gamma_{\mathbf{J}}
s do
not
depend on the choice of basepoint within the
K
-orbit, but
do
depend on the choice of
\mathbf{J}
. This allows us to avoid referring to relevant spaces as orbits of a preferred basepoint, and provide a geometric, basepoint free description of the orbit fibration maps.

In Theorem
2.12
, we also restate and provide a different proof of the incidence of the orbit closures (Theorem A.2 of
[
Tak68
]
, Theorem 10.6.3
[
Wol69
]
, Figure
A.2
) using a Hilsum-Skandalis bibundle of action Lie groupoids (Remark
2.13
).

We now discuss the correspondence between the Grassmannians
\operatorname{Gr}(\vec{n})
and the
G
-orbits
\operatorname{Lag}^{\mathbb{C}}(\vec{n})
.

In
[
Kim22
]
, it was observed that
\operatorname{Gr}(\vec{n})
and
\operatorname{Lag}^{\mathbb{C}}(\vec{n})
, are homotopy equivalent. For example, if
V=\mathbb{R}^{2}
with the standard symplectic form, the complex Lagrangian Grassmannian manifold is the complex projective line,
G=\operatorname{SL}(2;\mathbb{R})
and acts by the Möbius action, and its orbits are the upper hemisphere, equator, and the lower hemisphere. They correspond, respectively, to the point
\operatorname{Gr}(0,1,0;\mathbb{R}^{2}
), the real projective line
\operatorname{Gr}(1,0,0;\mathbb{R}^{2})
, and another point
\operatorname{Gr}(0,0,1;\mathbb{R}^{2})
(Figure
A.3
).

In this paper, we refine the correspondence into a correspondence of fiber bundles with contractible fibers and diffeomorphic base spaces. Moreover, we explain the reason why this correspondence exists at all. The reason is that the fibers of
\gamma_{\mathbf{J}}
and
\beta_{\mathbf{J}}
, which occur as bounded symmetric domains, are such that they have vector space manifestations that are complementary vector spaces. More precisely, we can consider the reduced case, which is fairly representative (Sections
3.3
,
3.4
). In this case
\operatorname{Ad}(\mathbf{J})
provides a Cartan decomposition of the Lie algebra of
G
into
\mathfrak{g}=\mathfrak{k}\oplus\mathfrak{p}
, and depending on the choice of
(0,n_{+},n_{-})
, we can additionally choose an involution
I
that commutes with
\mathbf{J}
, and further split
\mathfrak{p}
and
\mathfrak{g}
into
(\operatorname{Ad}(I),\operatorname{Ad}(\mathbf{J}))
-eigenspaces
\mathfrak{p}=\mathfrak{p}^{\shortparallel}\oplus\mathfrak{p}^{\times}
and
\mathfrak{g}=\mathfrak{k}^{\shortparallel}\oplus\mathfrak{k}^{\times}\oplus\mathfrak{p}^{\shortparallel}\oplus\mathfrak{p}^{\times}
. The exponential map provides the diffeomorphisms to the fibers of
\gamma_{\mathbf{J}}
,
\beta_{\mathbf{J}}
:

\displaystyle\mathfrak{p}^{\shortparallel}

\displaystyle\cong

\displaystyle(\operatorname{Sp}(2n_{+};\mathbb{R})/\operatorname{U}(n_{+}))\times(\operatorname{Sp}(2n_{-};\mathbb{R})/\operatorname{U}(n_{-}))

\displaystyle\mathfrak{p}^{\times}

\displaystyle\cong

\displaystyle\operatorname{U}(n_{+},n_{-})/(\operatorname{U}(n_{+})\times\operatorname{U}(n_{-})).

An intermediary fiber bundle

\eta_{\mathbf{J}}:\operatorname{Lag}_{\oplus}^{\mathbb{C}}(\vec{n};V)\to\operatorname{Lag}_{\mathbf{J},\oplus}^{\mathbb{C}}(\vec{n};V)

arises, as a fiber product of
\gamma_{\mathbf{J}}
and
\beta_{\mathbf{J}}
. The fibers of
\eta_{\mathbf{J}}
are diffeomorphic to
\mathfrak{p}
, and the base is diffeomorphic to
\operatorname{Gr}_{\mathbf{J}}(\vec{n};V)
and
\operatorname{Lag}_{\mathbf{J}}^{\mathbb{C}}(\vec{n};V)
. One of its projection maps carries a corrective diffeomorphism (Corollary
4.9
) of Baker-Campbell-Hausdorff nature which is the identity in the isotropic and coisotropic cases. In the symplectic case, the total space of
\eta_{\mathbf{J}}
was introduced in
[
LL11
]
as the
symplectic twistor Grassmannian
.

Finally, we list some observations that might interest some readers. We observe that the partial Cayley transforms introduced in
[
WK65
]
appear as elements of the binary octahedral group
BO_{48}
, or “square roots” of quaternionic symmetries. We note its formal similarities with the Bruhat decomposition of the complex Lagrangian Grassmannian (Section
2.6
). We also note the appearance of the Heisenberg groups of higher degree (appearing in the study of Jacobi forms
[
EZ85
]
,
[
Zie89
]
) in the stabilizer subgroups of each orbit (Section
2.2
). This allows us to describe them in a coordinate free way, and without referring to central extensions (Definition
2.6
). We also note the appearance of the unitary groups of every indefinite signature in the stabilizer subgroups of
\operatorname{Lag}^{\mathbb{C}}(V)
. We also marginally strengthen (Theorem
2.2
) the statement of the linear “relative Darboux theorem” in
[
AGN01
]
.

We discuss orbits in Section
2
, orbit fibrations, complex structures, and simultaneous linear symplectic reduction in Section
3
, and factorizations of orbit fibrations (the actual construction of
\gamma_{\mathbf{J}}
and
\beta_{\mathbf{J}}
) in Section
4
.

1.2
Notation

-1_{X}
denotes either the identity element of an object
X
or the identity automorphism of an object
X
.

Z(H)
denotes the center of a group
H
.
\operatorname{Lie}(H)
denotes the Lie algebra of a Lie group
H
. If
h\in H
, then
\operatorname{Ad}(h)(\cdot)=h(\cdot)h^{-1}
, and
\operatorname{ad}(\mathbf{h})(\cdot)=[\mathbf{h},\cdot]
denotes left multiplication by the multiplication in
\operatorname{Lie}(H)
by an element
\mathbf{h}\in\operatorname{Lie}(H)
.

For
R
, either the field of real or complex numbers,
\operatorname{Sp}(2n;R)
denotes
\operatorname{Sp}(R^{2n})
for
R^{2n}
equipped with the standard symplectic form.
\operatorname{U}(a,b)
denotes the indefinite unitary group.

\operatorname{Mat}_{j\times\ell}(R)
denotes the set of
j
by
\ell
matrices with coefficients in
R
,
1_{j}
the
j\times j
identity matrix,
0_{j\times\ell}\in\operatorname{Mat}_{j\times\ell}(R)
the zero matrix.
R^{n}
is identified with
\operatorname{Mat}_{n\times 1}(R)
.
(\cdot)^{t}
denotes the matrix transpose and
(\cdot)^{\dagger}
denotes the conjugate matrix tranpose,
\operatorname{Mat}_{n\times n}(\mathbb{C})^{\pm\dagger}
denotes the set of Hermitian/skew hermitian
n\times n
matrices and
\operatorname{Mat}_{n\times n}(R)^{\pm\mathfrak{t}}
denotes the set of symmetric/antisymmetric
n\times n
matrices.

For a Lie group
G
and a closed subgroup
H\leq G
and a smooth action of
H
on a space
F
,
G\times_{H}F:=(G\times F)/H
denote the orbit space of the action of
H
on
G\times F
given by
(g,f)\mapsto(gh,h^{-1}.f)
.

We often denote the bases given by Theorems
2.2
,
2.10
,
2.3
as
\{\mathbf{e}^{0},\mathbf{e}^{+},\mathbf{e}^{-},\mathbf{f}^{0},\mathbf{f}^{+},\mathbf{f}^{-}\}
,
\{\mathbf{e},\mathbf{f}\}
,
\{\mathbf{e},\mathbf{J}\mathbf{e}\}
.

2
Orbits

In this section we describe the relevant groups (including the binary octahedral group, and the Heisenberg group), spaces, group actions, orbits, and stabilizers. We also revisit some basis extension theorems (Theorems
2.2
,
2.3
,
2.10
), and describe the incidence relations between the orbit closures (Proposition
2.4
, Theorem
2.12
).

2.1
Orbits in ordinary real Grassmannians

Let
V
be a
2n
-dimensional real vector space and
\omega
be a symplectic form on
V
. Let
\operatorname{Sp}(V)
be the group of real linear invertible transformations of
V
that preserve
\omega
–i.e.
\omega(g\mathbf{v},g\mathbf{w})=\omega(\mathbf{v},\mathbf{w})
for all
\mathbf{v},\mathbf{w}\in V
if and only if
g\in\operatorname{Sp}(V)
. Suppose
W\subseteq V
is a real linear subspace of dimension
\ell
. Let
W^{\omega}\subseteq V
be defined by the condition
\mathbf{v}\in W^{\omega}
if and only if
\omega(\mathbf{v},\mathbf{w})=0
for all
\mathbf{w}\in W
and call it the
symplectic complement of
W
.
W
and
W^{\omega}
have complementary dimensions, but are not complementary subspaces in general. Taking the symplectic complement twice brings any linear subspace back to itself.

Denote
W_{0}:=W\cap W^{\omega}
and
W_{0}^{\omega}:=(W_{0})^{\omega}=W+W^{\omega}
.
\omega
descends to a nondegenerate form
\tilde{\omega}
on
W/W_{0}
, so
W/W_{0}
is a symplectic vector space with this form, and is in particular, even dimensional.
\omega
also descends to a symplectic form on
W^{\omega}/W_{0}
, which is again, necessarily even dimensional, and we will again denote the descended symplectic form by
\tilde{\omega}
. We will say
W
is of
type
\vec{n}
if
\dim_{\mathbb{R}}W_{0}=n_{0}
and
\frac{1}{2}\dim_{\mathbb{R}}W/W_{0}=n_{+}
. Every linear subspace of
V
has a type. Since
W
and
W^{\omega}
have complementary dimensions, the type of
W
determines the dimensions of
W
and
W^{\omega}
as
\dim_{\mathbb{R}}W=n_{0}+2n_{+}=\ell
and
\dim_{\mathbb{R}}W^{\omega}=n_{0}+2n_{-}=2n-\ell
, where
n_{-}:=n-n_{0}-n_{+}
. A subspace
W
is
isotropic
if and only if
n_{+}=0
,
coisotropic
if and only if
n_{-}=0
,
Lagrangian
if and only if
n_{0}=n
, and
symplectic
if and only if
n_{0}=0
.

Let
\operatorname{Gr}(\vec{n};V)=\operatorname{Gr}(\vec{n})
be the smooth manifold of linear subspaces of
V
of type
\vec{n}
. Let
\operatorname{Gr}(\ell;V)=\operatorname{Gr}(\ell;2n)
be the ordinary Grassmannian of
\ell
-dimensional real linear subspaces of
V
. We will view
\operatorname{Gr}(\vec{n};V)
as a submanifold of
\operatorname{Gr}(n_{0}+2n_{+};V)
. Then we have a partition

\operatorname{Gr}(\ell;V)=\bigsqcup_{\vec{n}:n_{0}+2n_{+}=\ell}\operatorname{Gr}(\vec{n};V)

by how subspaces behave with respect to the symplectic form. See Figure
A.1
for an example.

The linear action of
\operatorname{Sp}(V)
on
V
induces an action on each
\operatorname{Gr}(\vec{n};V)
by
g.W:=\{g\mathbf{w}:\mathbf{w}\in W\}
for
g\in\operatorname{Sp}(V)
and
W\in\operatorname{Gr}(\vec{n};V)
, for which only
\pm 1_{\operatorname{Sp}(V)}
act trivially. According to the linear “relative Darboux theorem” in Section 1.2 of
[
AGN01
]
), this action is transitive, so
\operatorname{Gr}(\vec{n};V)
’s are precisely the
\operatorname{Sp}(V)
-orbits of
\operatorname{Gr}(\ell;V)
. We will be using the following (slightly stronger) version of the linear “relative Darboux theorem.”

Definition 2.1

(Associated splittings)
.

Let
W\subseteq V
be a real linear subspace. We will call a triple
(W_{+},W_{-},W^{0})
of linear subspaces of
V
a
splitting of
V
associated to
W
if
W_{+}
is complementary to
W_{0}
in
W
,
W_{-}
is complementary to
W_{0}
in
W^{\omega}
, and
W^{0}
is an isotropic subspace of
V
complementary to
W_{0}
in
(W_{+}\oplus W_{-})^{\omega}
. Given
W_{+},W_{-}
, a choice of
W^{0}
is equivalent to a choice of quadratic form on
W_{0}
.

Theorem 2.2
.

Suppose
W\subseteq V
is a linear subspace of type
\vec{n}
and take any splitting
(W_{+},W_{-},W^{0})
associated to
W
. Then
(W_{\pm},\omega|_{W_{\pm}})
are symplectic subspaces of
V
, and there exists a Darboux basis

\{\mathbf{e}^{0}_{1},\cdots,\mathbf{e}^{0}_{n_{0}},\mathbf{e}^{+}_{1},\cdots,\mathbf{e}^{+}_{n_{+}},\mathbf{e}^{-}_{1},\cdots,\mathbf{e}^{-}_{n_{-}},\mathbf{f}^{0}_{1},\cdots,\mathbf{f}^{0}_{n_{0}},\mathbf{f}^{+}_{1},\cdots,\mathbf{f}^{+}_{n_{+}},\mathbf{f}^{-}_{1},\cdots,\mathbf{f}^{-}_{n_{-}}\}

of
V
such that
W_{0}=\operatorname{span}_{\mathbb{R}}\{\mathbf{e}^{0}_{j}\}_{1\leq j\leq n_{0}}
,
W^{0}=\operatorname{span}_{\mathbb{R}}\{\mathbf{f}^{0}_{j}\}_{1\leq j\leq n_{0}}
,
W_{+}=\operatorname{span}_{\mathbb{R}}\{\mathbf{e}^{+}_{\ell},\mathbf{f}^{+}_{\ell}\}_{1\leq\ell\leq n_{+}}
, and
W_{-}=\operatorname{span}_{\mathbb{R}}\{\mathbf{e}^{-}_{\ell},\mathbf{f}^{-}_{\ell}\}_{1\leq\ell\leq n_{-}}
.

Proof.

Consider the empty set as a Darboux basis of the zero vector space. Take a Darboux basis of
W/W_{0}
, and choose representatives
\{\tilde{\mathbf{e}}^{+}_{1},\cdots,\tilde{\mathbf{e}}^{+}_{n_{+}},\tilde{\mathbf{f}}^{+}_{1},\cdots,\tilde{\mathbf{f}}^{+}_{n_{+}}\}
in
W
. Since
W_{+}
is a complementary to
W_{0}
in
W
, we can uniquely decompose the vectors as

\displaystyle\tilde{\mathbf{e}}^{+}_{j}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\mathbf{e}^{+}_{j}+\mathbf{v}_{j}\quad\mathbf{e}^{+}_{j}\in W_{+},\mathbf{v}_{j}\in W_{0}

\displaystyle\tilde{\mathbf{f}}^{+}_{j}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\mathbf{f}^{+}_{j}+\mathbf{w}_{j}\quad\mathbf{f}^{+}_{j}\in W_{+},\mathbf{w}_{j}\in W_{0}

Then
\{\mathbf{e}^{+}_{1},\cdots,\mathbf{e}^{+}_{n_{+}},\mathbf{f}^{+}_{1},\cdots,\mathbf{f}^{+}_{n_{+}}\}
is a basis of
W_{+}
for which
\omega|_{W_{+}}
is nondegenerate, and is moreover a Darboux basis of
W_{+}
with respect to
\omega|_{W_{+}}
.
Similarly,
W_{-}
has a Darboux basis
\{\mathbf{e}^{-}_{1},\cdots,\mathbf{e}^{-}_{n_{-}},\mathbf{f}^{-}_{1},\cdots,\mathbf{f}^{-}_{n_{-}}\}
with respect to
\omega|_{W_{-}}
.
W_{0}\oplus W^{0}
is then a Lagrangian splitting of the symplectic vector space
(W_{+}\oplus W_{-})^{\omega}
. Applying Proposition 3.3 of
[
Kim21
]
, we get a Darboux basis
\{\mathbf{e}^{0}_{1},\cdots,\mathbf{e}^{0}_{n_{0}},\mathbf{f}^{0}_{1},\cdots,\mathbf{f}^{0}_{n_{0}}\}
of
(W_{+}\oplus W_{-})^{\omega}
such that
\{\mathbf{e}^{0}_{1},\cdots,\mathbf{e}^{0}_{n_{0}}\}
is a basis of
W_{0}
and
\{\mathbf{f}^{0}_{1},\cdots,\mathbf{f}^{0}_{n_{0}}\}
is a basis of
W^{0}
. Putting these Darboux bases together, we obtain the desired Darboux basis of
V
.
∎

Recall that a
linear complex structure

\mathbf{J}
on
V
is a real linear map on
V
such that
\mathbf{J}^{2}=-1_{V}
, and it is
\omega
-compatible
if
\mathbf{J}\in\operatorname{Sp}(V)
and
\omega(\mathbf{v},\mathbf{J}\mathbf{v})
is positive for every nonzero vector
\mathbf{v}
in
V
. We state another basis theorem when given an
\omega
-compatible linear complex structure
\mathbf{J}
.

Theorem 2.3
.

Suppose
\mathbf{J}
is an
\omega
-compatible linear complex structure on
V
.

1.

For any coisotropic subspace
W\subseteq V
of type
(n_{0},n_{+},0)
, there exists a Darboux basis
\{\mathbf{e}_{1},\cdots,\mathbf{e}_{n},\mathbf{J}\mathbf{e}_{1},\cdots,\mathbf{J}\mathbf{e}_{n}\}
of
V
such that

W^{\omega}=\operatorname{span}_{\mathbb{R}}\{\mathbf{e}_{j}\}_{1\leq j\leq n_{0}}\quad\text{and}\quad W=\operatorname{span}_{\mathbb{R}}\{\mathbf{e}_{j},\mathbf{J}\mathbf{e}_{\ell}\}_{1\leq j\leq n,n_{0}<\ell\leq n}.

2.

If
W\subseteq V
is a subspace such that
W\oplus\mathbf{J}W_{0}
is
\mathbf{J}
-invariant, the
\omega(\cdot,\mathbf{J}\cdot)
-orthogonal complement of
W_{0}
in
W
is
\mathbf{J}
-invariant and is the unique subspace of
W
that is both
\mathbf{J}
-invariant and complementary to
W_{0}
in
W
.

3.

If
W\subseteq V
is a subspace such that
W\oplus\mathbf{J}W_{0}
is
\mathbf{J}
-invariant then
W^{\omega}\oplus\mathbf{J}W_{0}
is also
\mathbf{J}
-invariant. Moreover, there exists an
\omega(\cdot,\mathbf{J}\cdot)
-orthogonal splitting

V=(W_{0}\oplus\mathbf{J}W_{0})\oplus W_{+}^{\mathbf{J}}\oplus W_{-}^{\mathbf{J}}

into
\mathbf{J}
-invariant symplectic subspaces of
V
such that

W=W_{0}\oplus W_{+}^{\mathbf{J}}
and
W^{\omega}=W_{0}\oplus W_{-}^{\mathbf{J}}
.
Equivalently, there exists a Darboux basis of
V
of the form
\{\mathbf{e}_{1},\cdots,\mathbf{e}_{n},\mathbf{J}\mathbf{e}_{1},\cdots,\mathbf{J}\mathbf{e}_{n}\}
such that

\displaystyle W_{0}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\operatorname{span}_{\mathbb{R}}\{\mathbf{e}_{j}\}_{1\leq j\leq n_{0}},

\displaystyle W

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\operatorname{span}_{\mathbb{R}}\{\mathbf{e}_{j},\mathbf{e}_{\ell},\mathbf{J}\mathbf{e}_{\ell}\}_{1\leq j\leq n_{0},n_{0}<\ell\leq n_{0}+n_{+}}

\displaystyle W^{\omega}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\operatorname{span}_{\mathbb{R}}\{\mathbf{e}_{j},\mathbf{e}_{\ell},\mathbf{J}\mathbf{e}_{\ell}\}_{1\leq j\leq n_{0},n_{0}+n_{+}<\ell\leq n}.

Proof.

(1)
: Take any splitting associated to
W
, and a Darboux basis given by Theorem
2.2
. By taking corresponding linear transformations of
\mathbf{f}
s, we may assume
\{\mathbf{e}_{j}^{0}\}_{1\leq j\leq n_{0}}
are
\omega(\cdot,\mathbf{J}\cdot)
-orthonormal. Let
\mathbf{e}_{j}:=\mathbf{e}_{j}^{0}
for
1\leq j\leq n_{0}
.

We can also assume
\{\mathbf{e}_{\ell}^{+}\}_{1\leq\ell\leq n_{+}}
are
\omega(\cdot,\mathbf{J}\cdot)
-orthogonal. Then for
1\leq\ell\leq n_{+}
, let

\tilde{\mathbf{e}}_{n_{0}+\ell}:=\mathbf{e}_{\ell}^{+}-\sum_{j=1}^{n_{0}}\omega(\mathbf{e}_{j}^{0},\mathbf{J}\mathbf{e}_{\ell}^{+})\mathbf{e}_{j}^{0}\in W.

Then
\mathbf{J}\tilde{\mathbf{e}}_{n_{0}+\ell}\in(W^{\omega})^{\omega}=W
.
Let
\mathbf{e}_{n_{0}+\ell}
and
\mathbf{J}\mathbf{e}_{n_{0}+\ell}
be the normalizations of
\tilde{\mathbf{e}}_{n_{0}+\ell}
and
\mathbf{J}\tilde{\mathbf{e}}_{n_{0}+\ell}
.

For the space
\operatorname{span}_{\mathbb{R}}\{\mathbf{e}_{j},\mathbf{J}\mathbf{e}_{j}\}_{1\leq j\leq n_{0}+n_{+}}
, its
\omega(\cdot,\mathbf{J}\cdot)
-orthogonal complement and its symplectic complement are equal. So the symplectic complement is a symplectic subspace with an
\omega(\cdot,\mathbf{J}\cdot)
-orthonormal Darboux basis
\{\mathbf{e}_{n_{0}+n_{+}+1},\cdots,\mathbf{e}_{n},\mathbf{J}\mathbf{e}_{n_{0}+n_{+}+1},\cdots,\mathbf{J}\mathbf{e}_{n}\}
. Then
\{\mathbf{e}_{1},\cdots,\mathbf{e}_{n},\mathbf{J}\mathbf{e}_{1},\cdots,\mathbf{J}\mathbf{e}_{n}\}
is the desired basis.
(2)
:
\omega
restricts to a symplectic form on
W\oplus\mathbf{J}W_{0}
. Then
W
is a coisotropic subspace of
W\oplus\mathbf{J}W_{0}
, and there exists a Darboux basis of
W\oplus\mathbf{J}W_{0}
given by (1). Then
W_{+}^{\mathbf{J}}:=\operatorname{span}_{\mathbb{R}}\{\mathbf{e}_{j},\mathbf{J}\mathbf{e}_{j}\}_{n_{0}<j\leq n-n_{-}}
is a subspace of
W
that is
\mathbf{J}
-invariant and complementary to
W_{0}
in
W
. Since
\mathbf{J}
is compatible with
\omega
, any subspace of
W
that is both
\mathbf{J}
-invariant and complementary to
W_{0}
in
W
coincides with both the symplectic complement and
\omega(\cdot,\mathbf{J}\cdot)
-orthogonal complement of
W_{0}\oplus\mathbf{J}W_{0}
in
W\oplus\mathbf{J}W_{0}
. Moreover, such a subspace coincides with the
\omega(\cdot,\mathbf{J}\cdot)
-orthogonal complement of
W_{0}
in
W
, and is thus unique. (3): Let
W_{+}^{\mathbf{J}}
be the subspace of
W
given by (2). Let
W_{-}^{\mathbf{J}}
be the
\omega(\cdot,\mathbf{J}\cdot)
-orthogonal complement of the
\mathbf{J}
-invariant subspace
W_{0}\oplus\mathbf{J}W_{0}\oplus W_{+}^{\mathbf{J}}
in
V
. Since
\mathbf{J}
is
\omega
compatible,
W_{-}^{\mathbf{J}}
is
\mathbf{J}
-invariant and contained in
W^{\omega}
. It is also transversal to
W_{0}\subseteq W^{\omega}
, so by counting dimensions
W^{\omega}=W_{0}\oplus W_{-}^{\mathbf{J}}
. Then
W^{\omega}\oplus\mathbf{J}W_{0}=(W_{0}\oplus\mathbf{J}W_{0})\oplus W_{-}^{\mathbf{J}}
and is
\mathbf{J}
-invariant. We obtain a splitting
(W_{+}^{\mathbf{J}},W_{-}^{\mathbf{J}},\mathbf{J}W_{0})
of
V
associated to
W
such that
W_{+}^{\mathbf{J}}
,
W_{-}^{\mathbf{J}}
are
\mathbf{J}
-invariant. Take a Darboux basis given by Theorem
2.2
, and we can replace the
\mathbf{f}^{+}
s and
\mathbf{f}^{-}
s by
\mathbf{J}\mathbf{e}^{+}
s and
\mathbf{J}\mathbf{e}^{-}
s to obtain the desired basis.
∎

The incidence relations of the closures of the Grassmannians
\operatorname{Gr}(\vec{n})
were stated in Section 2.2 of
[
LL11
]
:

Proposition 2.4
.

Suppose
n_{0}+n_{+}+n_{-}=n=n_{0}^{\prime}+n_{+}^{\prime}+n_{-}^{\prime}
and
n_{0}+2n_{+}=n_{0}^{\prime}+2n_{+}^{\prime}=\ell
. Then the following are equivalent

1.

\operatorname{Gr}(\vec{n}^{\prime};V)\cap\overline{\operatorname{Gr}(\vec{n};V)}
is nonempty.

2.

\operatorname{Gr}(\vec{n}^{\prime};V)\subseteq\overline{\operatorname{Gr}(\vec{n};V)}
.

3.

n_{+}^{\prime}\leq n_{+}
.

Proof.

(1)\iff(3)
: Consider the frame space
\operatorname{Fr}(\operatorname{Gr}(\ell;V))\subseteq V^{\ell}\times\operatorname{Gr}(\ell;V)
consisting of
((\mathbf{v}_{1},\cdots,\mathbf{v}_{\ell}),W)
such that
W=\operatorname{span}_{\mathbb{R}}\{\mathbf{v}_{1},\cdots,\mathbf{v}_{\ell}\}
. The map that sends
((\mathbf{v}_{1},\cdots,\mathbf{v}_{\ell}),W)
to the
\ell\times\ell
skew symmetric matrix
(\omega(\mathbf{v}_{j},\mathbf{v}_{k}))_{j,k}
is smooth. The map that sends a skew symmetric matrix to its eigenvalues takes values in the metric space of configurations of
\ell
unordered points in
i\mathbb{R}
that are symmetric about the origin. This space is stratified by how many points are zero and nonzero. By the sequence lifting property (Proposition 2.4 of
[
Siw71
]
) of these maps, any convergent sequence on the space of
\ell
unordered points lifts to a convergent sequence on
\operatorname{Fr}(\operatorname{Gr}(\ell;V))
. By the continuity of the the projection map
\operatorname{Fr}(\operatorname{Gr}(\ell;V))\to\operatorname{Gr}(\ell;V)
, any convergent sequence on
\operatorname{Fr}(\operatorname{Gr}(\ell;V))
descends to a convergence sequence on
\operatorname{Gr}(\ell;V)
. For the reverse direction, we note that all the maps involved are both continuous and have the sequence lifting property.
(1)\iff(2)
: This follows from the transitivity of the continuous
\operatorname{Sp}(V)
action.
∎

Corollary 2.5
.

\operatorname{Gr}(\vec{n};V)
is compact if and only if
n_{+}n_{-}=0
.

Proof.

By Proposition
2.4
, if
n_{+}
and
n_{-}
are both nonzero,
\operatorname{Gr}(\vec{n})
is not closed. The subgroup of all
g\in\operatorname{Sp}(V)
that commute with
\mathbf{J}
is compact, and by Theorem
2.3
acts transitively on
\operatorname{Gr}(\vec{n})
if
n_{+}n_{-}=0
.
∎

2.2
Orbits of the Heisenberg group

For a linear subspace
W\subseteq V
, let
\operatorname{Sp}_{W}(V)
denote the subgroup of linear transformations
g\in\operatorname{Sp}(V)
such that
g.W=W
. If
g\in\operatorname{Sp}_{W}(V)
,
g.W^{\omega}=W^{\omega}
, so
\operatorname{Sp}_{W}(V)=\operatorname{Sp}_{W^{\omega}}(V)
. Moreover,
g.W_{0}=W_{0}
so
g
descends to symplectic linear transformations of
W/W_{0}
and
W^{\omega}/W_{0}
, which we denote by
\left[g\right]
and
\left[g\right]^{\omega}
, respectively.

Definition 2.6

(Heisenberg group)
.

Suppose
W\subseteq V
is a real subspace. Let
H(W)\leq\operatorname{Sp}_{W}(V)
be the subgroup of symplectic linear transformations
g
of
V
such that
g
restricts to the identity transformation on
W_{0}
and
g
descends to the identity transformation on both
W/W_{0}
and
W^{\omega}/W_{0}
. Denote by
Z(W)\leq H(W)
the subgroup of symplectic linear transformations that restrict to the identity transformation on both
W
and
W^{\omega}
.

Suppose
\{\mathbf{e},\mathbf{f}\}
is a basis given by Theorem
2.2
for some
W
of type
\vec{n}
and associated splitting
(W_{+},W_{-},W^{0})
. In the reordered basis
\{\mathbf{e}^{0},\mathbf{e}^{+},\mathbf{f}^{+},\mathbf{e}^{-},\mathbf{f}^{-},\mathbf{f}^{0}\}
, elements of
H(W)
take the block form

\begin{pmatrix}1_{n_{0}}&E_{+}&F_{+}&E_{-}&F_{-}&Y\\
0&1_{n_{+}}&0&0&0&F_{+}^{t}\\
0&0&1_{n_{+}}&0&0&-E_{+}^{t}\\
0&0&0&1_{n_{-}}&0&F_{-}^{t}\\
0&0&0&0&1_{n_{-}}&-E_{-}^{t}\\
0&0&0&0&0&1_{n_{0}}\end{pmatrix},

(3)

where
1_{k}
is the
k\times k
identity matrix and

Y-E_{+}F_{+}^{t}-E_{-}F_{-}^{t}\in\operatorname{Mat}_{n_{0}\times n_{0}}(\mathbb{R})^{t}.

(4)

The matrix form shows
H(W)
is nilpotent, contractible, and

\dim_{\mathbb{R}}H(W)=2n_{0}n_{+}+2n_{0}n_{-}+\frac{1}{2}n_{0}(n_{0}+1).

(5)

H(W)
is trivial if
W
is symplectic, abelian if
W
is Lagrangian and nonabelian otherwise. From the definition, we can also immediately see
H(W)=H(W^{\omega})
, and
Z(W)=Z(W^{\omega})=Z(W+W^{\omega})=Z(W_{0})
.

The matrix form in Equation (
3
) shows that
Z(W)
is the center of
H(W)
. In a Darboux basis obtained from any splitting associated to
W
,
Z(W)
consists of elements such that
E_{\pm}=F_{\pm}=0_{n_{0}\times n_{\pm}}
. Since
W^{0}
can be identified with the dual space of
W_{0}
from the symplectic form,
Z(W)
can be identified with the abelian group of quadratic forms on
W_{0}
under addition. In particular,
\dim_{\mathbb{R}}Z(W)=n_{0}(n_{0}+1)/2
.
H(W)/Z(W)
is isomorphic to the abelian group of real linear maps from
W_{0}
to
W_{0}^{\omega}/W_{0}
under addition
(\operatorname{Hom}_{\mathbb{R}}(W_{0},W_{0}^{\omega}/W_{0}),+)
. So
H(W)
can be viewed as a central extension of groups

Z(W)\hookrightarrow H(W)\twoheadrightarrow\operatorname{Hom}_{\mathbb{R}}(W_{0},W_{0}^{\omega}/W_{0}).

(6)

By taking a Darboux basis change of
W_{+}
and
W_{-}
if necessary, for a choice of Lagrangian subspaces
L_{+}\subseteq W_{+}
and
L_{-}\subseteq W_{-}
, the abelian groups
(\operatorname{Hom}_{\mathbb{R}}(W_{0},L_{\pm}),+)
embed into
H(W)
, where their images are given, respectively, by the vanishing of
F_{+}
,
E_{-}
,
F_{-}
, and
Y
, and by the vanishing of
E_{+}
,
F_{+}
,
F_{-}
and
Y
. Then we have a split group extension

Z(W)\times\operatorname{Hom}_{\mathbb{R}}(W_{0},L_{+}\oplus L_{-})\hookrightarrow H(W)\twoheadrightarrow\operatorname{Hom}_{\mathbb{R}}(W_{0},W_{+}/L_{+}\oplus W_{-}/L_{-}).

(7)

In the usual Darboux basis
\{\mathbf{e}^{0},\mathbf{e}^{+},\mathbf{e}^{-},\mathbf{f}^{0},\mathbf{f}^{+},\mathbf{f}^{-}\}
,
g\in H(W)
takes the block form

\begin{pmatrix}1_{n_{0}}&E_{+}&E_{-}&Y&F_{+}&F_{-}\\
0&1_{n_{+}}&0&F_{+}^{t}&0&0\\
0&0&1_{n_{-}}&F_{-}^{t}&0&0\\
0&0&0&1_{n_{0}}&0&0\\
0&0&0&-E_{+}^{t}&1_{n_{+}}&0\\
0&0&0&-E_{-}^{t}&0&1_{n_{-}}\end{pmatrix}.

(8)

We will denote the matrix group of
2n\times 2n
matrices in this block form and satisfying Equation (
4
) as
H(\vec{n})
.

Remark 2.7

(Symmetries of Jacobi forms of higher degree)
.

A particular matrix form of
H(W)
has been referred to as
the Heisenberg group
in
[
Zie89
]
to define Jacobi forms of higher degree. In the Darboux basis
\{\mathbf{e}^{+},\mathbf{e}^{-},\mathbf{e}^{0},\mathbf{f}^{+},\mathbf{f}^{-},\mathbf{f}^{0}\}
,
g\in H(\vec{n})
takes the block matrix form

\begin{pmatrix}1_{n_{+}}&0&0&0&0&F_{+}^{t}\\
0&1_{n_{-}}&0&0&0&F_{-}^{t}\\
E_{+}&E_{-}&1_{n_{0}}&F_{+}&F_{-}&Y\\
0&0&0&1_{n_{+}}&0&-E_{+}^{t}\\
0&0&0&0&1_{n_{-}}&-E_{-}^{t}\\
0&0&0&0&0&1_{n_{0}}\end{pmatrix}

(9)

and we can identify with Ziegler’s notation for
H_{\mathbb{R}}^{(n_{+}+n_{-},n_{0})}
by
\lambda=\begin{pmatrix}E_{+}&E_{-}\end{pmatrix}
,
\mu=\begin{pmatrix}F_{+}&F_{-}\end{pmatrix}
, and
x=-Y
.

Suppose
g\in\operatorname{Sp}_{W}(V)
, and
\{\mathbf{e},\mathbf{f}\}
is a Darboux basis provided by Theorem
2.2
. In the
\{\mathbf{e}^{0},\mathbf{e}^{+},\mathbf{f}^{+},\mathbf{e}^{-},\mathbf{f}^{-},\mathbf{f}^{0}\}
basis,
g
takes the block matrix form

\begin{pmatrix}\ast&\ast&\ast&\ast&\ast&\ast\\
0_{n_{+}\times n_{0}}&\ast&\ast&0_{n_{+}\times n_{-}}&0_{n_{+}\times n_{-}}&\ast\\
0_{n_{+}\times n_{0}}&\ast&\ast&0_{n_{+}\times n_{-}}&0_{n_{+}\times n_{-}}&\ast\\
0_{n_{-}\times n_{0}}&0_{n_{-}\times n_{+}}&0_{n_{-}\times n_{+}}&\ast&\ast&\ast\\
0_{n_{-}\times n_{0}}&0_{n_{-}\times n_{+}}&0_{n_{-}\times n_{+}}&\ast&\ast&\ast\\
0_{n_{0}\times n_{0}}&0_{n_{0}\times n_{+}}&0_{n_{0}\times n_{+}}&0_{n_{0}\times n_{-}}&0_{n_{0}\times n_{-}}&\ast\end{pmatrix}

This matrix can be decomposed as a product of two matrices in block form:

\begin{pmatrix}X&0&0&0&0&0\\
0&A_{+}&B_{+}&0&0&0\\
0&C_{+}&D_{-}&0&0&0\\
0&0&0&A_{-}&B_{-}&0\\
0&0&0&C_{-}&D_{-}&0\\
0&0&0&0&0&(X^{t})^{-1}\end{pmatrix}\begin{pmatrix}1_{n_{0}}&E_{+}&F_{+}&E_{-}&F_{-}&Y\\
0&1_{n_{+}}&0&0&0&F_{+}^{t}\\
0&0&1_{n_{+}}&0&0&-E_{+}^{t}\\
0&0&0&1_{n_{-}}&0&F_{-}^{t}\\
0&0&0&0&1_{n_{-}}&-E_{-}^{t}\\
0&0&0&0&0&1_{n_{0}}\end{pmatrix},

where, the assumption that
g
is symplectic is equivalent to

\left(X,\begin{pmatrix}A_{+}&B_{+}\\
C_{+}&D_{+}\end{pmatrix},\begin{pmatrix}A_{-}&B_{-}\\
C_{-}&D_{-}\end{pmatrix}\right)\in\operatorname{GL}(n_{0};\mathbb{R})\times\operatorname{Sp}(2n_{+};\mathbb{R})\times\operatorname{Sp}(2n_{-};\mathbb{R}),

and
Y-E_{+}F_{+}^{t}-E_{-}F_{-}^{t}\in\operatorname{Mat}_{n_{0}\times n_{0}}(\mathbb{R})^{t}
. From this observation we can deduce the following:

Proposition 2.8
.

If
W\subseteq V
is a real subspace, the set of splittings of
V
associated to
W
is a principal homogeneous space for
H(W)
. The set of pairs of splittings
\{(W_{+},W_{-}):W=W_{0}\oplus W_{+},W^{\omega}=W_{0}\oplus W_{-}\}
is a principal homogeneous space for
H(W)/Z(W)
.

Proof.

For two splittings
(W_{+},W_{-},W^{0})
and
(W_{+}^{\prime},W_{-}^{\prime},(W^{0})^{\prime})
of
V
associated to
W
, obtain two Darboux bases
\{\mathbf{e},\mathbf{f}\}
and
\{\mathbf{e}^{\prime},\mathbf{f}^{\prime}\}
by Theorem
2.2
. The linear extension of the map that sends
\{\mathbf{e},\mathbf{f}\}
to
\{\mathbf{e}^{\prime},\mathbf{f}^{\prime}\}
is a symplectic linear transformation that stabilizes
W
. Express it in the
\{\mathbf{e}^{0},\mathbf{e}^{+},\mathbf{f}^{+},\mathbf{e}^{-},\mathbf{f}^{-},\mathbf{f}^{0}\}
basis. Then taking the upper triangular part of the matrix decomposition, we obtain a unique element of
H(W)
that maps
(W_{+},W_{-},W^{0})
to
(W_{+}^{\prime},W_{-}^{\prime},(W^{0})^{\prime})
.
H(W)
acts on
\{(W_{+},W_{-})\}
by
g.(W_{+},W_{-})=(g.W_{+},g.W_{-})
with kernel
Z(W)
.
∎

\operatorname{Sp}_{W}(V)
can be written as a semidirect product in different ways.

Theorem 2.9
.

Suppose
(W_{+},W_{-},W^{0})
is a splitting of
V
associated to
W
. Then the group extensions

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces H(W)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\operatorname{Sp}_{W}(V)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\operatorname{GL}(W_{0})\times\operatorname{Sp}(W/W_{0})\times\operatorname{Sp}(W^{\omega}/W_{0})}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\scriptscriptstyle\cdot|_{W_{0}}\times[\cdot]\times[\cdot]^{\omega}}}

(10)

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces(\operatorname{GL}(W_{0})\times\operatorname{Sp}(W_{-}))\ltimes H(W)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\operatorname{Sp}_{W}(V)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\operatorname{Sp}(W/W_{0})}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\scriptscriptstyle[\cdot]}}

(11)

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces(\operatorname{Sp}(W_{+})\times\operatorname{Sp}(W_{-}))\ltimes H(W)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\operatorname{Sp}_{W}(V)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\operatorname{GL}(W_{0})}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\cdot|_{W_{0}}}}

(12)

are right split.

Proof.

Using a Darboux basis given by Theorem
2.2
, we can identify
\operatorname{Sp}(W_{+})\cong\operatorname{Sp}(W/W_{0})
and
\operatorname{Sp}(W_{-})\cong\operatorname{Sp}(W^{\omega}/W_{0})
and identify the elements with their matrix representations. The splitting homomorphism of the extension (
10
) maps

\left(X,\begin{pmatrix}A_{+}&B_{+}\\
C_{+}&D_{+}\end{pmatrix},\begin{pmatrix}A_{-}&B_{-}\\
C_{-}&D_{-}\end{pmatrix}\right)\in\operatorname{GL}(W_{0})\times\operatorname{Sp}(W_{+})\times\operatorname{Sp}(W_{-})

to

\begin{pmatrix}X&0&0&0&0&0\\
0&A_{+}&0&0&B_{+}&0\\
0&0&A_{-}&0&0&B_{-}\\
0&0&0&(X^{t})^{-1}&0&0\\
0&C_{+}&0&0&D_{+}&0\\
0&0&C_{-}&0&0&D_{-}\end{pmatrix}\in\operatorname{Sp}(V).

The other splitting homomorphisms are obtained by restricting the components of
\operatorname{GL}(W_{0})\times\operatorname{Sp}(W/W_{0})\times\operatorname{Sp}(W^{\omega}/W_{0})
to identity elements.
∎

2.3
Orbits in complex Lagrangian Grassmannians

For a real vector space
W
, denote its complexification
\mathbb{C}\otimes_{\mathbb{R}}W
by
W^{\mathbb{C}}
. Write an elementary tensor as
a\otimes_{\mathbb{R}}\mathbf{w}
of
W^{\mathbb{C}}
as
a\mathbf{w}
, and denote its complex conjugate
\overline{a}\otimes_{\mathbb{R}}\mathbf{w}
by
\overline{a\mathbf{w}}
or
\overline{a}\mathbf{w}
. Extend complex conjugation linearly to an
\mathbb{R}
-linear involution on
W^{\mathbb{C}}
and denote the complex conjugate of
\mathbf{v}\in V^{\mathbb{C}}
by
\overline{\mathbf{v}}
. For a linear transformation
g
of
V^{\mathbb{C}}
, denote its complex conjugate
\overline{g}\mathbf{v}:=\overline{g\overline{\mathbf{v}}}
.

Let
\omega^{\mathbb{C}}
be the complex bilinear extension of
\omega
to
V^{\mathbb{C}}
. Let
\operatorname{Sp}(V^{\mathbb{C}})
be the group of complex invertible linear transformations of
V^{\mathbb{C}}
that preserve
\omega^{\mathbb{C}}
.
\operatorname{Sp}(V)
embeds into
\operatorname{Sp}(V^{\mathbb{C}})
by scalar extension of linear transformations, and identify
\operatorname{Sp}(V)
as a subgroup of
\operatorname{Sp}(V^{\mathbb{C}})
.

Call a complex subspace
\mathbf{F}\subseteq V^{\mathbb{C}}

a complex Lagrangian subspace
if it has complex dimension
n
and
\omega^{\mathbb{C}}|_{\mathbf{F}\otimes_{\mathbb{C}}\mathbf{F}}
vanishes identically. Denote the space of all complex Lagrangian subspaces of
V^{\mathbb{C}}
by
\operatorname{Lag}^{\mathbb{C}}(V)
and call it
the complex Lagrangian Grassmannian
. If we choose an identification of
V^{\mathbb{C}}
with
\mathbb{C}^{2n}
,
\operatorname{Sp}(V^{\mathbb{C}})
is identified with a linear algebraic group, so
\operatorname{Lag}^{\mathbb{C}}(V)
becomes a a (complex) projective variety with complex dimension
n(n+1)/2
. If we choose an
\omega
-compatible linear complex structure
\mathbf{J}
,
\operatorname{Lag}^{\mathbb{C}}(V)
becomes a Kähler manifold (Remark
3.4
), and in fact, a hermitian symmetric space.

\operatorname{Sp}(V)
acts on
\operatorname{Lag}^{\mathbb{C}}(V)
by
g.\mathbf{F}:=\{g\mathbf{v}:\mathbf{v}\in\mathbf{F}\}
for which only
\pm 1_{\operatorname{Sp}(V)}
acts trivially. This action can be viewed as a generalization of the Möbius action of
\operatorname{SL}(2;\mathbb{R})
on the Riemann sphere (Section
A.2
).

\kappa(\mathbf{v},\mathbf{w}):=-i\omega^{\mathbb{C}}(\mathbf{v},\overline{\mathbf{w}})
is a hermitian form with split signature
(n,n)
on
V^{\mathbb{C}}
. It restricts to a hermitian form on any complex subspace of
V^{\mathbb{C}}
, in particular for any complex Lagrangian subspace
\mathbf{F}
.

As before, let
\vec{n}:=(n_{0},n_{+},n_{-})
be an ordered triple of nonnegative integers that sum to
n
. We will call
\mathbf{F}
a (complex) Lagrangian subspace of
type
\vec{n}
if
\kappa|_{\mathbf{F}}
has signature (inertia)
(n_{0},n_{+},n_{-})
. Let
\mathbf{F}_{0}
be the kernel of
\kappa|_{\mathbf{F}}
and we will call
(\mathbf{F},\mathbf{F}_{+},\mathbf{F}_{-})
a
splitting of
\mathbf{F}
if
\mathbf{F}=\mathbf{F}_{0}\oplus\mathbf{F}_{+}\oplus\mathbf{F}_{-}
and both
\kappa|_{\mathbf{F}_{+}}
and
-\kappa|_{\mathbf{F}_{-}}
are positive definite. The null cone of
\mathbf{F}
,
\{\mathbf{v}\in\mathbf{F}:\kappa(\mathbf{v},\mathbf{v})=0\}
is a cartesian product of
\mathbf{F}_{0}
and the null cone of
\mathbf{F}_{+}\oplus\mathbf{F}_{-}
,
\{\mathbf{v}\in\mathbf{F}_{+}\oplus\mathbf{F}_{-}:\kappa(\mathbf{v},\mathbf{v})=0\}
.

Let
\operatorname{Lag}^{\mathbb{C}}(\vec{n};V)=\operatorname{Lag}^{\mathbb{C}}(\vec{n})
denote the set of complex Lagrangian subspaces of
V^{\mathbb{C}}
of type
\vec{n}
. Since every
g\in\operatorname{Sp}(V)
preserves
\kappa
, the generalized Möbius action stabilizes every
\operatorname{Lag}^{\mathbb{C}}(\vec{n})
. In fact, the
(n+1)(n+2)/2
elements of
\{\operatorname{Lag}^{\mathbb{C}}(\vec{n})\}_{\vec{n}}
are precisely the orbits of
\operatorname{Sp}(V)
in
\operatorname{Lag}^{\mathbb{C}}(V)
. Since any linear map that takes a Darboux basis to a Darboux basis is symplectic, the transitivity of the generalized Möbius action is a consequence of the following basis theorem.

Theorem 2.10

(Lemma 5.1 of
[
BR86
]
)
.

Suppose
\mathbf{F}
is a complex Lagrangian subspace of
V^{\mathbb{C}}
of type
\vec{n}
with splitting
\mathbf{F}=\mathbf{F}_{0}\oplus\mathbf{F}_{+}\oplus\mathbf{F}_{-}
. Then there exists a Darboux basis of
\mathbb{R}^{2n}

\{\mathbf{e}^{0}_{1},\cdots,\mathbf{e}^{0}_{n_{0}},\mathbf{e}^{+}_{1},\cdots,\mathbf{e}^{+}_{n_{+}},\mathbf{e}^{-}_{1},\cdots,\mathbf{e}^{-}_{n_{-}},\mathbf{f}^{0}_{1},\cdots,\mathbf{f}^{0}_{n_{0}},\mathbf{f}^{+}_{1},\cdots,\mathbf{f}^{+}_{n_{+}},\mathbf{f}^{-}_{1},\cdots,\mathbf{f}^{-}_{n_{-}}\}

such that
\mathbf{F}_{0}=\operatorname{span}_{\mathbb{C}}\{\mathbf{e}^{0}_{j}\}_{1\leq j\leq n_{0}}
,
\mathbf{F}_{+}=\operatorname{span}_{\mathbb{C}}\{\mathbf{e}^{+}_{k}-i\mathbf{f}^{+}_{k}\}_{1\leq k\leq n_{+}}
, and
\mathbf{F}_{-}=\operatorname{span}_{\mathbb{C}}\{\mathbf{e}^{-}_{\ell}+i\mathbf{f}^{-}_{\ell}\}_{1\leq\ell\leq n_{-}}
.

Proof.

In the original reference the proof is left as an exercise for the reader. We refer to Theorem 2.3.14, Proposition 2.6.5 of
[
Kim22
]
for explicit proofs.
∎

Remark 2.11

(The Real Lagrangian Grassmannian)
.

As a corollary,
\operatorname{Lag}^{\mathbb{C}}(n,0,0;V)
can be identified with image of the embedding
\operatorname{Gr}(n,0,0;V)\xhookrightarrow{\mathbb{C}\otimes_{\mathbb{R}}\cdot}\operatorname{Lag}^{\mathbb{C}}(V)
.

The incidence relations between the orbit closures are referred to as the Orbit Structure Theorem in
[
Wol72
]
, and is also stated in Theorem A.2 of
[
Tak68
]
, Theorem 10.6.3 of
[
Wol69
]
(c.f. Boundary Orbit theorem of
[
Wol72
]
). An example is shown in Figure
A.2
. We provide a different proof in the specific case we are considering.

Theorem 2.12

(Special case of Theorem 10.6.3 of
[
Wol69
]
, Theorem A.2 of
[
Tak68
]
)
.

Suppose
n_{0}+n_{+}+n_{-}=n_{0}^{\prime}+n_{+}^{\prime}+n_{-}^{\prime}=n
. The following are equivalent

1.

\operatorname{Lag}^{\mathbb{C}}(\vec{n}^{\prime};V)\cap\overline{\operatorname{Lag}^{\mathbb{C}}(\vec{n};V)}
is nonempty.

2.

\operatorname{Lag}^{\mathbb{C}}(\vec{n}^{\prime};V)\subseteq\overline{\operatorname{Lag}^{\mathbb{C}}(\vec{n};V)}
.

3.

n_{-}^{\prime}\leq n_{-}
and
n_{+}^{\prime}\leq n_{+}
.

Proof.

(1)\iff(3)
: Consider the frame space
\operatorname{Fr}(\operatorname{Lag}^{\mathbb{C}}(V))\subseteq(V^{\mathbb{C}})^{n}\times\operatorname{Lag}^{\mathbb{C}}(V)
consisting of
((\mathbf{v}_{1},\cdots,\mathbf{v}_{n}),\mathbf{F})
such that
\mathbf{F}=\operatorname{span}_{\mathbb{C}}\{\mathbf{v}_{1},\cdots,\mathbf{v}_{n}\}
.

The map that assigns to a frame
((\mathbf{v}_{1},\cdots,\mathbf{v}_{n}),\mathbf{F})
the
n\times n
hermitian matrix
(\kappa(\mathbf{v}_{j},\mathbf{v}_{\ell}))_{j,\ell}
is smooth, and the map that takes an
n\times n
hermitian matrix to its eigenvalues is also continuous. The eigenvalues take values in the metric space of
n
unordered points on
\mathbb{R}
, which is stratified by how many points are positive, zero, and negative. In this space, one stratum has a nonempty intersection with the closure of another if and only if it does not have more positive points and more negative points than the other.

As in the proof of Theorem
2.4
, this stratification is “pulled back” to a stratification on
\operatorname{Fr}(\operatorname{Lag}^{\mathbb{C}}(V))
via the continuity and sequence lifting property (cf. Proposition 2.4
[
Siw71
]
) of the aforementioned maps. It is “pushed forward” to the desired stratification on
\operatorname{Lag}^{\mathbb{C}}(V)
via the continuity and sequence lifting property of the projection map
((\mathbf{v}_{1},\cdots,\mathbf{v}_{n}),\mathbf{F})\mapsto\mathbf{F}
.
(1)\iff(2)
: This follows from the transitivity of the continuous action of
\operatorname{Sp}(V)
.
∎

Remark 2.13

(Frame space as a Hilsum-Skandalis bibundle)
.

We first notice that a smooth
G
-equivariant map
\pi:E\to B
consists of three pieces of data that happily satisfy a compatibility condition: the smooth action of
G
on
E
, the smooth action of
G
on
B
, and the map
\pi
. We can view this object from a groupoid perpective by switching the order in which the data is given. We first construct an action Lie groupoid
G\times B\rightrightarrows B
from the action of
G
on
B
, and then observe
\pi
is an anchor map of the action (cf. Definition 3.22 of
[
Ler10
]
) of the (action) Lie groupoid
G\times B\rightrightarrows B
on
E
. This also holds for
G
-equivariant maps for right actions of
G
on
E
and
B
.

The frame space
\operatorname{Fr}(\operatorname{Lag}^{\mathbb{C}}(V))
constructed in the proof of Theorem
2.12
admits a right
\operatorname{GL}(n;\mathbb{C})
action by taking linear combinations of the
\mathbf{v}_{j}
s and a left
\operatorname{Sp}(V)
action acting diagonally on all components. The projection to
\operatorname{Lag}^{\mathbb{C}}(V)
is
\operatorname{Sp}(V)
-equivariant and
\operatorname{GL}(n;\mathbb{C})
-invariant and they commute. From a groupoid perspective, one can check
\operatorname{Fr}(\operatorname{Lag}^{\mathbb{C}}(V))
is a
(Hilsum-Skandalis) bibundle
(Definition 3.25 of
[
Ler10
]
)

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\operatorname{Sp}(V)\times\operatorname{Lag}^{\mathbb{C}}(V)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\operatorname{Fr}(\operatorname{Lag}^{\mathbb{C}}(V))}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\operatorname{GL}(n;\mathbb{C})\times\operatorname{Mat}_{n\times n}(\mathbb{C})^{\dagger}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\operatorname{Lag}^{\mathbb{C}}(V)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\operatorname{Mat}}_{n\times n}(\mathbb{C})^{\dagger}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces a_{L}}
\circlearrowright
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces a_{R}}
\circlearrowleft

(13)

The rightmost column is the action Lie groupoid of the action of
\operatorname{GL}(n;\mathbb{C})
on
\operatorname{Mat}_{n\times n}(\mathbb{C})^{\dagger}
by
g^{\dagger}Ag
, and
a_{R}(\mathbf{v}_{1},\cdots,\mathbf{v}_{n})=(\kappa(\mathbf{v}_{j},\mathbf{v}_{k}))_{j,k}
is the anchor map of its right action on
\operatorname{Fr}(\operatorname{Lag}^{\mathbb{C}}(V))
. The leftmost column is the action Lie groupoid of the generalized Möbius action.
a_{L}(\mathbf{v}_{1},\cdots,\mathbf{v}_{n})=\operatorname{span}_{\mathbb{C}}\{\mathbf{v}_{1},\cdots,\mathbf{v}_{n}\}
is the anchor map of its left action on
\operatorname{Fr}(\operatorname{Lag}^{\mathbb{C}}(V))
.
a_{L}
is also a principal bundle (cf. Definition 3.17 of
[
Ler10
]
) for the groupoid in the rightmost column, and
a_{R}
is invariant with respect to the left action of the groupoid in the leftmost column, making
\operatorname{Fr}(\operatorname{Lag}^{\mathbb{C}}(V))
a (Hilsum-Skandalis) bibundle.

Remark 2.14
.

Since
\operatorname{Sp}(V^{\mathbb{C}})
is simple, our notation and Wolf’s notation in the Orbit Structure Theorem of
[
Wol72
]
(also Theorem 10.6.3 of
[
Wol69
]
) are identified via
|\Psi|=n
,
|\Gamma|=n_{0}
, and

n_{+}=|\Sigma\setminus\Gamma|\leq|\Sigma|\leq|\Sigma\cup\Gamma|=n_{0}+n_{+}.

In the Boundary Orbit Theorem of
[
Wol72
]
,
|\Gamma|=n_{+}
.
We refer to Appendix
A.4.4
,
A.4.5
for details.

2.4
Real projections of complex Lagrangian subspaces

Consider the projection map
\operatorname{Re}(\mathbf{v}):=(\mathbf{v}+\overline{\mathbf{v}})/2
from
V^{\mathbb{C}}
to
V
along
iV
. As a consequence of Theorem
2.10
, for a complex Lagrangian subspace
\mathbf{F}

\displaystyle(\operatorname{Re}\mathbf{F}_{0})^{\mathbb{C}}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\mathbf{F}_{0}

(14)

\displaystyle\operatorname{Re}\mathbf{F}_{0}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle(\operatorname{Re}\mathbf{F})^{\omega}\subseteq\operatorname{Re}\mathbf{F}.

\operatorname{Re}\mathbf{F}
is coisotropic, so
\omega|_{\operatorname{Re}\mathbf{F}}
descends to a symplectic form on
\operatorname{Re}\mathbf{F}/(\operatorname{Re}\mathbf{F})^{\omega}
. Moreover,
\mathbf{F}+\overline{\mathbf{F}}
is the
\omega^{\mathbb{C}}
-symplectic complement of
\mathbf{F}_{0}
, so
\omega^{\mathbb{C}}|_{\mathbf{F}+\overline{\mathbf{F}}}
descends to a complex bilinear symplectic form on
(\mathbf{F}+\overline{\mathbf{F}})/\mathbf{F}_{0}
. So

(\mathbf{F}+\overline{\mathbf{F}})/\mathbf{F}_{0}=(\operatorname{Re}\mathbf{F})^{\mathbb{C}}/((\operatorname{Re}\mathbf{F})^{\omega})^{\mathbb{C}}\cong(\operatorname{Re}\mathbf{F}/(\operatorname{Re}\mathbf{F})^{\omega})^{\mathbb{C}}

is an identification of complexified symplectic vector spaces.

Remark 2.15

(Polarizations)
.

When
n=1
, a point in
\operatorname{Lag}^{\mathbb{C}}(\mathbb{R}^{2})
can be viewed as a polarization of a ray of light (as an equivalence class of Jones vectors). In the literature of geometric quantization (eg Section 4.1 of
[
Śni80
]
Section V.3 of
[
GS90
]
, Section 5.4 of
[
Woo91
]
), distributions of the complexified tangent bundle of a symplectic manifold fiberwise satisfying the complex Lagrangian condition has been referred to as
polarizations
. When
n_{-}=0
the following construction was used on the level of spaces, but not of the elements:

\displaystyle\operatorname{Re}\mathbf{F}_{0}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\mathbf{D}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle V\cap\mathbf{F}\cap\overline{\mathbf{F}}

\displaystyle\operatorname{Re}\mathbf{F}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\mathbf{E}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle V\cap(\mathbf{F}+\overline{\mathbf{F}}).

For a complex Lagrangian subspace
\mathbf{F}
, let
\operatorname{Sp}_{\mathbf{F}}(V)
denote the subgroup of linear transformations
g\in\operatorname{Sp}(V)
such that
g.\mathbf{F}=\mathbf{F}
. Since
\operatorname{Re}(g\mathbf{v})=g\operatorname{Re}(\mathbf{v})
for all
\mathbf{v}\in V^{\mathbb{C}}
,
\operatorname{Sp}_{\mathbf{F}}(V)
is a subgroup of
\operatorname{Sp}_{\operatorname{Re}\mathbf{F}}(V)
. Choose a splitting
\mathbf{F}=\mathbf{F}_{0}\oplus\mathbf{F}_{+}\oplus\mathbf{F}_{-}
, and observing how
H(\operatorname{Re}\mathbf{F})
acts on the Darboux basis provided by Theorem
2.10
, we have
H(\operatorname{Re}\mathbf{F})\leq\operatorname{Sp}_{\mathbf{F}}(V)\leq\operatorname{Sp}_{\operatorname{Re}\mathbf{F}}(V)
.

If
g\in\operatorname{Sp}_{\mathbf{F}}(V)
, then
g.\mathbf{F}_{0}=\mathbf{F}_{0}
, and
g.\operatorname{Re}\mathbf{F}_{0}=\operatorname{Re}\mathbf{F}_{0}
, so
g|_{\operatorname{Re}\mathbf{F}_{0}}\in\operatorname{GL}(\operatorname{Re}\mathbf{F}_{0})
. Moreover,
\kappa|_{\mathbf{F}}
descends to a nondegenerate mixed signature hermitian form on
\mathbf{F}/\mathbf{F}_{0}
. Denote by
\operatorname{U}(\mathbf{F}/\mathbf{F}_{0})
the automorphisms of this hermitian vector space. Then
g\in\operatorname{Sp}_{\mathbf{F}}(V)
descends to a unitary transformation on
\mathbf{F}/\mathbf{F}_{0}
that we denote by
[g]_{0}
.

As before,
\operatorname{Sp}_{\mathbf{F}}(V)
can be written as a semidirect product in different ways.

Theorem 2.16
.

Suppose
\mathbf{F}
is a complex Lagrangian subspace. The group extensions

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces H(\operatorname{Re}\mathbf{F})}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\operatorname{Sp}_{\mathbf{F}}(V)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\operatorname{GL}(\operatorname{Re}\mathbf{F}_{0})\times\operatorname{U}(\mathbf{F}/\mathbf{F}_{0})}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\cdot|_{\operatorname{Re}\mathbf{F}_{0}}\times{[\cdot]_{0}}}}

(15)

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\operatorname{GL}(\operatorname{Re}\mathbf{F}_{0})\ltimes H(\operatorname{Re}\mathbf{F})}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\operatorname{Sp}_{\mathbf{F}}(V)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\operatorname{U}(\mathbf{F}/\mathbf{F}_{0})}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces[\cdot]_{0}}

(16)

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\operatorname{U}(\mathbf{F}/\mathbf{F}_{0})\ltimes H(\operatorname{Re}\mathbf{F})}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\operatorname{Sp}_{\mathbf{F}}(V)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\operatorname{GL}(\operatorname{Re}\mathbf{F}_{0})}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\cdot|_{\operatorname{Re}\mathbf{F}_{0}}}

(17)

are right split.

Proof.

Choose a splitting
\mathbf{F}=\mathbf{F}_{0}\oplus\mathbf{F}_{+}\oplus\mathbf{F}_{-}
and take a Darboux basis
\{\mathbf{e},\mathbf{f}\}
given by Theorem
2.10
. Since
\operatorname{Sp}_{\mathbf{F}}(V)\leq\operatorname{Sp}_{\operatorname{Re}\mathbf{F}}(V)
, in the
\{\mathbf{e}^{0},\mathbf{e}^{+},\mathbf{e}^{-},\mathbf{f}^{+},\mathbf{f}^{-},\mathbf{f}^{0}\}
basis,
g\in\operatorname{Sp}_{\mathbf{F}}(V)
can be expressed as a product
g^{\prime}\cdot h
where
(g^{\prime})_{\{\mathbf{e}^{0},\mathbf{e}^{+},\mathbf{e}^{-},\mathbf{f}^{+},\mathbf{f}^{-},\mathbf{f}^{0}\}}
has block form

\begin{pmatrix}X&0&0&0&0&0\\
0&A_{++}&A_{+-}&B_{++}&B_{+-}&0\\
0&A_{-+}&A_{--}&B_{-+}&B_{--}&0\\
0&C_{++}&C_{+-}&D_{++}&D_{+-}&0\\
0&C_{+-}&C_{--}&D_{-+}&D_{--}&0\\
0&0&0&0&0&(X^{t})^{-1}\end{pmatrix}

(18)

and
(h)_{\{\mathbf{e}^{0},\mathbf{e}^{+},\mathbf{e}^{-},\mathbf{f}^{+},\mathbf{f}^{-},\mathbf{f}^{0}\}}
has block form

\begin{pmatrix}1_{n_{0}}&E_{+}&E_{-}&F_{+}&F_{-}&Y\\
0&1_{n_{+}}&0&0&0&F_{+}^{t}\\
0&0&1_{n_{+}}&0&0&F_{-}^{t}\\
0&0&0&1_{n_{-}}&0&-E_{+}^{t}\\
0&0&0&0&1_{n_{-}}&-E_{-}^{t}\\
0&0&0&0&0&1_{n_{0}}\end{pmatrix}.

(19)

Since
g.\mathbf{F}=\mathbf{F}
, in the complex Darboux basis whose first
n
vectors span
\mathbf{F}

\left\{\mathbf{e}^{0},\frac{1}{\sqrt{2}}(\mathbf{e}^{+}-i\mathbf{f}^{+}),-\frac{i}{\sqrt{2}}(\mathbf{e}^{-}+i\mathbf{f}^{-}),-\frac{i}{\sqrt{2}}(\mathbf{e}^{+}+i\mathbf{f}^{+}),\frac{1}{\sqrt{2}}(\mathbf{e}^{-}-i\mathbf{f}^{-}),\mathbf{f}^{0}\right\}

the lower left
n\times n
block of the matrix form of
g^{\prime}
, which is equal to

\frac{i}{2}\begin{pmatrix}0&0&0\\
0&A_{++}-D_{++}&A_{+-}+D_{+-}\\
0&-A_{-+}-D_{-+}&-A_{--}+D_{--}\end{pmatrix}+\frac{1}{2}\begin{pmatrix}0&0&0\\
0&B_{++}+C_{++}&-B_{+-}+C_{+-}\\
0&-B_{-+}+C_{-+}&B_{--}+C_{--}\end{pmatrix},

vanishes. This implies that the upper left
n\times n
block of the matrix form of
g^{\prime}
, acting unitarily on
(\mathbf{F},\kappa|_{\mathbf{F}})
, is

\begin{pmatrix}X&0&0\\
0&A_{++}-iB_{++}&A_{+-}+iB_{+-}\\
0&A_{-+}-iB_{-+}&A_{--}+iB_{--}\end{pmatrix}=\begin{pmatrix}X&0&0\\
0&D_{++}+iC_{++}&-D_{+-}+iC_{+-}\\
0&-D_{-+}-iC_{-+}&D_{--}+iC_{--}\end{pmatrix}.

(20)

The vanishing of the lower left
n\times n
block of the matrix form of
h
gives no additional constraints, so the upper left
n\times n
block of the matrix form of
h
acts unitarily on
(\mathbf{F},\kappa|_{\mathbf{F}})
as

\begin{pmatrix}1_{n_{0}}&\frac{1}{\sqrt{2}}(E_{+}-iF_{+})&\frac{1}{\sqrt{2}}(E_{-}+iF_{-})\\
0&1_{n_{+}}&0\\
0&0&1_{n_{-}}\end{pmatrix}.

If we take
g\in\operatorname{U}(\mathbf{F}/\mathbf{F}_{0})
, in the basis taken from the image of

\left\{\frac{1}{\sqrt{2}}(\mathbf{e}^{+}-i\mathbf{f}^{+}),-\frac{i}{\sqrt{2}}(\mathbf{e}^{-}+i\mathbf{f}^{-}),-\frac{i}{\sqrt{2}}(\mathbf{e}^{+}+i\mathbf{f}^{+}),\frac{1}{\sqrt{2}}(\mathbf{e}^{-}-i\mathbf{f}^{-})\right\}

under the quotient map, it has block form

\begin{pmatrix}g_{++}&g_{+-}\\
g_{-+}&g_{--}\end{pmatrix}.

Then the splitting morphism takes

\left(X,g\right)\in\operatorname{GL}(\operatorname{Re}\mathbf{F}_{0})\times\operatorname{U}(\mathbf{F}/\mathbf{F}_{0})

to

\begin{pmatrix}X&0&0&0&0&0\\
0&\operatorname{Re}g_{++}&\operatorname{Re}g_{+-}&0&-\operatorname{Im}g_{++}&\operatorname{Im}g_{+-}\\
0&\operatorname{Re}g_{-+}&\operatorname{Re}g_{--}&0&-\operatorname{Im}g_{-+}&\operatorname{Im}g_{--}\\
0&0&0&(X^{t})^{-1}&0&0\\
0&\operatorname{Im}g_{++}&\operatorname{Im}g_{+-}&0&\operatorname{Re}g_{++}&-\operatorname{Re}g_{+-}\\
0&-\operatorname{Im}g_{-+}&-\operatorname{Im}g_{--}&0&-\operatorname{Re}g_{-+}&\operatorname{Re}g_{--}\end{pmatrix}

(21)

in the Darboux basis
\{\mathbf{e},\mathbf{f}\}
.
The other splitting morphisms can be obtained by letting
g:=1_{\operatorname{U}(\mathbf{F}/\mathbf{F}_{0})}
and
X:=1_{\operatorname{Re}\mathbf{F}_{0}}
.
∎

Remark 2.17
.

When
n_{-}=0
,
\operatorname{Sp}_{\mathbf{F}}(V)
has been computed in Proposition 3.3 of
[
Hes06
]
.

2.5
Equivalence classes of splittings

Definition 2.18

(Equivalent splittings)
.

Let two splittings
(\mathbf{F},\mathbf{F}_{+},\mathbf{F}_{-})
and
(\mathbf{F},\mathbf{F}^{\prime}_{+},\mathbf{F}^{\prime}_{-})
of a complex Lagrangian subspace
\mathbf{F}
be
equivalent
if
\mathbf{F}_{0}\oplus\mathbf{F}_{+}=\mathbf{F}^{\prime}_{0}\oplus\mathbf{F}^{\prime}_{+}
and
\mathbf{F}_{0}\oplus\mathbf{F}_{-}=\mathbf{F}^{\prime}_{0}\oplus\mathbf{F}^{\prime}_{-}
. Equivalently, two splittings are equivalent if they descend to the same splitting on the quotient spaces
\mathbf{F}/\mathbf{F}_{0}
and
\mathbf{F}/\mathbf{F}^{\prime}_{0}
.
Denote an equivalence class of splittings by

\mathbf{F}^{\oplus}:=[(\mathbf{F},\mathbf{F}_{+},\mathbf{F}_{-})].

(22)

Let
\mathbf{F}^{\oplus}_{\geq 0}:=\mathbf{F}_{0}\oplus\mathbf{F}_{+}
, and
\mathbf{F}^{\oplus}_{\leq 0}:=\mathbf{F}_{0}\oplus\mathbf{F}_{-}
, so that we can express
\mathbf{F}^{\oplus}
as a pair
(\mathbf{F}^{\oplus}_{\geq 0},\mathbf{F}^{\oplus}_{\leq 0})
.
We will also denote by

\mathbf{F}^{\oplus}=[\mathbf{F}_{0}\oplus\mathbf{F}_{+}\oplus\mathbf{F}_{-}]

when we assume a choice of representative of the equivalence class has been made. Let
\operatorname{Lag}^{\mathbb{C}}_{\oplus}(\vec{n})
denote the set of equivalence classes of complex Lagrangian subspaces of type
\vec{n}
with splittings. Let
\operatorname{Lag}^{\mathbb{C}}_{\oplus}(V)
be the space of all equivalence classes of complex Lagrangian subspaces of
V
with splittings.

By Theorem
2.10
,
\operatorname{Re}\mathbf{F}^{\oplus}_{\geq 0}
and
\operatorname{Re}\mathbf{F}^{\oplus}_{\leq 0}
are symplectic complements of each other, so
H(\operatorname{Re}\mathbf{F}^{\oplus}_{\geq 0})=H(\operatorname{Re}\mathbf{F}^{\oplus}_{\leq 0})
.

Denote the linear symplectic reduction

\mathbf{F}^{\oplus}/\mathbf{F}_{0}:=[(\mathbf{F}/\mathbf{F}_{0},\mathbf{F}^{\oplus}_{\geq 0}/\mathbf{F}_{0},\mathbf{F}^{\oplus}_{\leq 0}/\mathbf{F}_{0})]\in\operatorname{Lag}_{\oplus}^{\mathbb{C}}(0,n_{+},n_{-};\operatorname{Re}\mathbf{F}^{\oplus}_{\geq 0}/\operatorname{Re}\mathbf{F}_{0}).

Remark 2.19

(Splittings as eigenspaces)
.

Given an
\omega
-compatible linear complex structure
\mathbf{J}
, for every complex Lagrangian subspace
\mathbf{F}
there is a linear map
\kappa|_{\mathbf{F}}^{\sharp}:\mathbf{F}\to\mathbf{F}
such that

\kappa|_{\mathbf{F}}(\mathbf{u},\mathbf{v})=\omega^{\mathbb{C}}(\mathbf{u},\mathbf{J}\overline{\kappa|_{\mathbf{F}}^{\sharp}\mathbf{v}})\quad\mathbf{u},\mathbf{v}\in\mathbf{F}.

Equivalently,
\omega^{\mathbb{C}}(\cdot,\mathbf{J}\overline{\cdot})
restricts to a positive definite inner product on
\mathbf{F}
, so
\kappa|_{\mathbf{F}}
gets identified with
\kappa|_{\mathbf{F}}^{\sharp}
under
\mathbf{F}^{\vee}\otimes\mathbf{F}^{\vee}\xrightarrow{\cong}\mathbf{F}\otimes\mathbf{F}^{\vee}
where
\mathbf{F}^{\vee}
is the dual space of
\mathbf{F}
as a real vector space.

For
\mathbf{F}\in\operatorname{Lag}^{\mathbb{C}}(\vec{n})
,
\mathbf{J}
gives a preferred choice of splitting
\mathbf{F}=\mathbf{F}_{0}\oplus\mathbf{F}_{+}\oplus\mathbf{F}_{-}
by the zero, positive, and negative eigenspaces of
\kappa|_{\mathbf{F}}^{\sharp}
, and a preferred choice of equivalence class of splittings
\iota_{\mathbf{J}}(\mathbf{F}):=[\mathbf{F}_{0}\oplus\mathbf{F}_{+}\oplus\mathbf{F}_{-}]
. So we obtain a section
\iota_{\mathbf{J}}:\operatorname{Lag}^{\mathbb{C}}(\vec{n})\hookrightarrow\operatorname{Lag}^{\mathbb{C}}_{\oplus}(\vec{n})
of the forgetful fibration
\varphi:\mathbf{F}^{\oplus}\mapsto\mathbf{F}
.

Remark 2.20

(Symplectic Twistor Grassmannians of
[
LL11
]
)
.

\operatorname{Lag}^{\mathbb{C}}(0,n_{+},n_{-})
can be identified with the
symplectic Twistor Grassmannians

\hat{\mathcal{T}}(2n_{+},2n)
introduced in Definition 15.ii of
[
LL11
]
. If
\mathbf{F}^{\oplus}=[0\oplus\mathbf{F}_{+}\oplus\mathbf{F}_{-}]\in\operatorname{Lag}^{\mathbb{C}}(0,n_{+},n_{-})
, then
\operatorname{Re}|_{\mathbf{F}_{\pm}}:\mathbf{F}_{\pm}\to\operatorname{Re}\mathbf{F}_{\pm}
are real vector space isomorphisms. Then
\mathbf{J}_{\operatorname{Re}\mathbf{F}_{\pm}}\mathbf{v}:=\operatorname{Re}(\pm i\operatorname{Re}|_{\mathbf{F}_{\pm}}^{-1}(\mathbf{v}))
are
\omega|_{\operatorname{Re}\mathbf{F}_{\pm}}
-compatible linear complex structures on
\operatorname{Re}\mathbf{F}_{\pm}
. So
(\operatorname{Re}\mathbf{F}_{+},\mathbf{J}_{\operatorname{Re}\mathbf{F}_{+}},\mathbf{J}_{\operatorname{Re}\mathbf{F}_{-}})
is an element of the symplectic twistor space
\hat{\mathcal{T}}(2n_{+},2n)
.
Conversely, if
(W,\mathbf{J}_{W},\mathbf{J}_{W^{\omega}})\in\hat{\mathcal{T}}(2n_{+},2n)
, then let
\mathbf{F}_{\mathbf{J}_{W}}
be the
+i
-eigenspace of
\mathbf{J}_{W}
in
W^{\mathbb{C}}
and
\mathbf{F}_{\mathbf{J}_{W^{\omega}}}
be the
-i
-eigenspace of
\mathbf{J}_{W^{\omega}}
of
\mathbf{J}_{W^{\omega}}
in
(W^{\omega})^{\mathbb{C}}
. Then
\mathbf{F}_{\mathbf{J}_{W}}\oplus\mathbf{F}_{\mathbf{J}_{W^{\omega}}}
is a splitting of a complex Lagrangian subspace of
(W\oplus W^{\omega})^{\mathbb{C}}=V^{\mathbb{C}}
.

Theorem 2.21
.

The group extensions

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces H(\operatorname{Re}\mathbf{F}_{\geq 0}^{\oplus})}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\operatorname{Sp}_{\mathbf{F}^{\oplus}}(V)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\operatorname{GL}(\operatorname{Re}\mathbf{F}_{0})\times\operatorname{U}(\mathbf{F}^{\oplus}_{\geq 0}/\mathbf{F}_{0})\times\operatorname{U}(\mathbf{F}^{\oplus}_{\leq 0}/\mathbf{F}_{0})}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\cdot|_{\operatorname{Re}\mathbf{F}_{0}}\times[\cdot]_{+}\times[\cdot]_{-}}}

(23)

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces(\operatorname{GL}(\operatorname{Re}\mathbf{F}_{0})\times\operatorname{U}(\mathbf{F}^{\oplus}_{\leq 0}/\mathbf{F}_{0}))\ltimes H(\operatorname{Re}\mathbf{F}_{\geq 0}^{\oplus})}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\operatorname{Sp}_{\mathbf{F}^{\oplus}}(V)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\operatorname{U}(\mathbf{F}^{\oplus}_{\geq 0}/\mathbf{F}_{0})}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces[\cdot]_{+}}

(24)

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces(\operatorname{U}(\mathbf{F}^{\oplus}_{\geq 0}/\mathbf{F}_{0})\times\operatorname{U}(\mathbf{F}^{\oplus}_{\leq 0}/\mathbf{F}_{0}))\ltimes H(\operatorname{Re}\mathbf{F}_{\geq 0}^{\oplus})}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\operatorname{Sp}_{\mathbf{F}^{\oplus}}(V)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\operatorname{GL}(\operatorname{Re}\mathbf{F}_{0})}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\cdot|_{\operatorname{Re}\mathbf{F}_{0}}}

(25)

are right split.

Proof.

We repeat the proof of Theorem
2.16
with
A_{\pm\mp}
,
B_{\pm\mp}
,
C_{\pm\mp}
,
D_{\pm\mp}
,
g_{\pm\mp}
set to zero.
∎

Remark 2.22

(Dimensions)
.

If
W
and
\mathbf{F}
are of type
\vec{n}
, by choosing Darboux bases given by Theorems
2.2
,
2.10
, there exist group isomorphisms (
[
Kim22
]
Proposition 2.7.4)

\displaystyle\operatorname{Sp}_{\mathbf{F}^{\oplus}}(V)

\displaystyle\cong

\displaystyle(\operatorname{GL}(n_{0};\mathbb{R})\times\operatorname{U}(n_{+})\times\operatorname{U}(n_{-}))\ltimes H(\vec{n})

\displaystyle\operatorname{Sp}_{\mathbf{F}}(V)

\displaystyle\cong

\displaystyle(\operatorname{GL}(n_{0};\mathbb{R})\times\operatorname{U}(n_{+},n_{-}))\ltimes H(\vec{n})

\displaystyle\operatorname{Sp}_{W}(V)

\displaystyle\cong

\displaystyle(\operatorname{GL}(n_{0};\mathbb{R})\times\operatorname{Sp}(2n_{+};\mathbb{R})\times\operatorname{Sp}(2n_{-};\mathbb{R}))\ltimes H(\vec{n}).

So

\displaystyle\dim_{\mathbb{R}}\operatorname{Sp}_{\mathbf{F}^{\oplus}}(V)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle n^{2}+\frac{n_{0}(n_{0}+1)}{2}-2n_{+}n_{-}

\displaystyle\dim_{\mathbb{R}}\operatorname{Sp}_{\mathbf{F}}(V)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle n^{2}+\frac{n_{0}(n_{0}+1)}{2}

\displaystyle\dim_{\mathbb{R}}\operatorname{Sp}_{W}(V)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle n^{2}+\frac{n_{0}(n_{0}+1)}{2}+(n_{+}-n_{-})^{2}+n_{+}+n_{-}.

Considering
W
,
\mathbf{F}
, and
\mathbf{F}^{\oplus}
as basepoints we obtain diffeomorphisms

\displaystyle\operatorname{Lag}_{\oplus}^{\mathbb{C}}(\vec{n};V)

\displaystyle\cong

\displaystyle\operatorname{Sp}(V)/\operatorname{Sp}_{\mathbf{F}^{\oplus}}(V).

\displaystyle\operatorname{Lag}^{\mathbb{C}}(\vec{n};V)

\displaystyle\cong

\displaystyle\operatorname{Sp}(V)/\operatorname{Sp}_{\mathbf{F}}(V).

\displaystyle\operatorname{Gr}(\vec{n};V)

\displaystyle\cong

\displaystyle\operatorname{Sp}(V)/\operatorname{Sp}_{W}(V).

From this we compute

\displaystyle\dim_{\mathbb{R}}\operatorname{Lag}^{\mathbb{C}}_{\oplus}(\vec{n};V)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle n^{2}+n-\frac{n_{0}(n_{0}+1)}{2}+2n_{+}n_{-}

\displaystyle\dim_{\mathbb{R}}\operatorname{Lag}^{\mathbb{C}}(\vec{n};V)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle n^{2}+n-\frac{n_{0}(n_{0}+1)}{2}

\displaystyle\dim_{\mathbb{R}}\operatorname{Gr}(\vec{n};V)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle n^{2}+n-\frac{n_{0}(n_{0}+1)}{2}-(n_{+}-n_{-})^{2}-n_{+}-n_{-}.

2.6
Cayley transforms and binary octahedral symmetries

In this section we will closely examine the case
V=\mathbb{R}^{2}
with standard symplectic form
\omega_{\mathbb{R}^{2}}
. We identify
V^{\mathbb{C}}
with
\mathbb{C}^{2}
, equipped with the standard hermitian inner product
\langle\cdot,\cdot\rangle_{\mathbb{C}^{2}}
. The linear action of
\operatorname{SL}(2;\mathbb{C})
on
\mathbb{C}^{2}
factors through
\{\pm 1\}
on
\mathbb{P}^{1}(\mathbb{C})
. Consequently, we have

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\operatorname{SU}(2)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\operatorname{SL}(2;\mathbb{C})}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathbb{C}^{2}\setminus\{(0,0)\}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\operatorname{SO}(3)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\operatorname{PSL}(2;\mathbb{C})}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathbb{P}^{1}(\mathbb{C}).}
\circlearrowright
\circlearrowright

Now consider the Pauli matrices

\sigma_{1}:=\begin{pmatrix}0&1\\
1&0\end{pmatrix}\quad\sigma_{2}:=\begin{pmatrix}0&-i\\
i&0\end{pmatrix}\quad\sigma_{3}:=\begin{pmatrix}1&0\\
0&-1\end{pmatrix}.

(26)

Then let
\mathbf{i}:=-i\sigma_{1}
,
\mathbf{j}:=-i\sigma_{2}
,
\mathbf{k}:=-i\sigma_{3}
. As elements of the matrix group
\operatorname{SU}(2)
,
\mathbf{i}
,
\mathbf{j}
,
\mathbf{k}
satisfy the quaternionic relations

\mathbf{i}\mathbf{j}=\mathbf{k}\quad\mathbf{j}\mathbf{k}=\mathbf{i}\quad\mathbf{k}\mathbf{i}=\mathbf{j},\quad\mathbf{i}^{2}=\mathbf{j}^{2}=\mathbf{k}^{2}=-1,

and define a hyperkähler structure on
\mathbb{C}^{2}
, and
\mathbf{j}
is an
\omega_{\mathbb{R}^{2}}
-compatible linear complex structure on
\mathbb{R}^{2}
. The quaternion group
Q_{8}:=\{\pm 1,\pm\mathbf{i},\pm\mathbf{j},\pm\mathbf{k}\}\leq\operatorname{SU}(2)
is a nonsplit double cover of the Klein four group
K_{4}\cong Q_{8}/\{\pm 1\}
. As elements of
\operatorname{SU}(2)
, we can consider the square roots

\displaystyle\sqrt{\mathbf{i}}

\displaystyle:=

\displaystyle\frac{1}{\sqrt{2}}(1+\mathbf{i})=\frac{1}{\sqrt{2}}\begin{pmatrix}1&-i\\
-i&1\end{pmatrix}

\displaystyle\sqrt{\mathbf{j}}

\displaystyle:=

\displaystyle\frac{1}{\sqrt{2}}(1+\mathbf{j})=\frac{1}{\sqrt{2}}\begin{pmatrix}1&-1\\
1&1\end{pmatrix}

\displaystyle\sqrt{\mathbf{k}}

\displaystyle:=

\displaystyle\frac{1}{\sqrt{2}}(1+\mathbf{k})=\frac{1}{\sqrt{2}}\begin{pmatrix}1-i&0\\
0&1+i\end{pmatrix}.

\sqrt{\mathbf{i}}
,
\sqrt{\mathbf{j}}
,
\sqrt{\mathbf{k}}
are elements of
\operatorname{SU}(2)
and in particular,
the linear fractional transformation associated to
\sqrt{\mathbf{i}}
is the Cayley transform
. For our discussion, we observe that the cyclic group of order four
\langle\sqrt{\mathbf{i}}\rangle/\langle-1_{\operatorname{SU}(2)}\rangle
permutes the following real lines
