(27)

and is responsible for relating Theorems
2.2
and
2.10
.

\sqrt{\mathbf{i}}
and
\mathbf{j}
generate the binary octahedral group
BO_{48}
which is a nonsplit double cover of the octahedral group
O_{24}
. So the octahedral and binary octahedral groups occur as “square roots” of the quaternionic symmetries in the following manner

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces Q_{8}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces BO_{48}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\operatorname{SU}(2)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces K_{4}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces O_{24}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\operatorname{SO}(3).}

(28)

Note that complex conjugation and the antipodal map do not preserve the orientation of
\mathbb{P}^{1}(\mathbb{C})
and do not appear within this diagram.
The elements

\mathbf{O}:=\{[0:1],[1:0],[i:1],[-i:1],[1:1],[-1:1]\}

are vertices of a regular octahedron in
\mathbb{P}^{1}(\mathbb{C})\cong S^{2}
.
\sqrt{\mathbf{i}}
,
\sqrt{\mathbf{j}}
,
\sqrt{\mathbf{k}}
act on
\mathbb{P}^{1}(\mathbb{C})
by a quarter rotation about, respectively, the axes
[-1:1]-[1:1]
,
[i:1]-[-i:1]
,
[0:1]-[1:0]
.
\mathbf{i}
,
\mathbf{j}
,
\mathbf{k}
act by a half rotation about the respective axes, and the element

\sqrt{\mathbf{i}}\sqrt{\mathbf{j}}=\frac{1}{2}(1+\mathbf{i}+\mathbf{j}+\mathbf{k})=\frac{1}{2}\begin{pmatrix}1-i&-1-i\\
1-i&1+i\end{pmatrix}

is one of the elements that permute the three axes.

With respect to the standard Hermitian product on
\mathbb{C}^{2}
, we can identify the Pauli matrices with hermitian forms on
\mathbb{C}^{2}
by

\langle\sigma_{j}\mathbf{u},\mathbf{v}\rangle_{\mathbb{C}^{2}}=\overline{\mathbf{v}}^{t}\sigma_{j}\mathbf{u}\quad\mathbf{u},\mathbf{v}\in\mathbb{C}^{2},j=1,2,3.

Let
\operatorname{U}(\sigma_{j})
be the unitary group associated to the hermitian form defined by
\sigma_{j}
, for
j=1,2,3
. Since
\sqrt{\mathbf{i}}
,
\sqrt{\mathbf{j}}
,
\sqrt{\mathbf{k}}\in\operatorname{SU}(2)
, their conjugate transpose is equal to their inverse. Moreover,

\sqrt{\mathbf{i}}\cdot\sigma_{2}\cdot\sqrt{\mathbf{i}}^{-1}=\sigma_{3},\quad\sqrt{\mathbf{j}}\cdot\sigma_{3}\cdot\sqrt{\mathbf{j}}^{-1}=\sigma_{1},\quad\sqrt{\mathbf{k}}\cdot\sigma_{1}\cdot\sqrt{\mathbf{k}}^{-1}=\sigma_{2},

so the following three real forms of
\operatorname{SL}(2;\mathbb{C})
are mapped to each other by

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\operatorname{SL}(2;\mathbb{C})\cap\operatorname{U}(\sigma_{1})}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\operatorname{SL}(2;\mathbb{C})\cap\operatorname{U}(\sigma_{2})}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\operatorname{SL}(2;\mathbb{C})\cap\operatorname{U}(\sigma_{3}).}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\operatorname{Ad}_{\sqrt{\mathbf{k}}}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\operatorname{Ad}_{\sqrt{\mathbf{i}}}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\operatorname{Ad}_{\sqrt{\mathbf{j}}}}

(29)

Here
\operatorname{SL}(2;\mathbb{C})\cap\operatorname{U}(\sigma_{2})=\operatorname{SL}(2;\mathbb{R})
and
\operatorname{SL}(2;\mathbb{C})\cap\operatorname{U}(\sigma_{3})=\operatorname{SU}(1,1)
.

An
\mathfrak{sl}_{2}
triple can be constructed by

\left(\frac{i\mathbf{i}-\mathbf{j}}{2},i\mathbf{k},\frac{i\mathbf{i}+\mathbf{j}}{2}\right)=\left(\begin{pmatrix}0&1\\
0&0\end{pmatrix},\begin{pmatrix}1&0\\
0&-1\end{pmatrix},\begin{pmatrix}0&0\\
1&0\end{pmatrix}\right),

(30)

Iterating the 4-periodic
\operatorname{Ad}_{\sqrt{\mathbf{i}}}
on this
\mathfrak{sl}_{2}
triple, we obtain mappings between the root space decompositions of
\mathfrak{sl}(2;\mathbb{R})
and
\mathfrak{su}(1,1)
:

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathbb{R}\begin{pmatrix}i&&1\\
1&&-i\end{pmatrix}\oplus\mathbb{R}\begin{pmatrix}0&&i\\
-i&&0\end{pmatrix}\oplus\mathbb{R}\begin{pmatrix}-i&&1\\
1&&i\end{pmatrix}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathbb{R}\begin{pmatrix}0&&0\\
1&&0\end{pmatrix}\oplus\mathbb{R}\begin{pmatrix}-1&&0\\
0&&1\end{pmatrix}\oplus\mathbb{R}\begin{pmatrix}0&&1\\

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathbb{R}\begin{pmatrix}0&&1\\

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathbb{R}\begin{pmatrix}-i&&1\\

1&&-i\end{pmatrix}.}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\operatorname{Ad}_{\sqrt{\mathbf{i}}}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\operatorname{Ad}_{\sqrt{\mathbf{i}}}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\operatorname{Ad}_{\sqrt{\mathbf{i}}}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\operatorname{Ad}_{\sqrt{\mathbf{i}}}}

Here
\operatorname{Ad}_{\sqrt{\mathbf{i}}}^{2}
is the unique nontrivial Weyl group symmetry associated to the Cartan subalgebras
\mathbb{C}\mathbf{j}
and
\mathbb{C}\mathbf{k}
of
\mathfrak{sl}(2;\mathbb{C})
.

The
\mathbb{R}^{2n}
case is obtained by taking
n
direct products of these structures. We refer the reader to Section
A.4.2
for how these structures appear from a traditional Lie theoretic perspective.

2.7
Extension of Weyl group symmetries

Now fix a Darboux basis
\{\mathbf{e},\mathbf{f}\}
of
V
and identify
V\cong(\mathbb{R}^{2})^{n}
with
\{\mathbf{e},\mathbf{f}\}
. Let
H
be a discrete subgroup of
\operatorname{SU}(2)
,
\mathbb{S}_{n}
be the permutation group of
\{1,\cdots,n\}
and choose
\ast:=(\mathbf{e}_{1},\cdots,\mathbf{e}_{n})\in(\mathbb{R}^{2})^{n}
and denote
L_{\ast}:=\operatorname{span}_{\mathbb{R}}\{\mathbf{e}_{1},\cdots,\mathbf{e}_{n}\}
. Then this choice picks out a discrete subset

F_{H,\ast}:=\{\operatorname{span}_{\mathbb{C}}\{\mathbf{w}_{1},\cdots,\mathbf{w}_{n}\}:\mathbf{w}_{j}\in H\mathbb{R}\mathbf{e}_{j},1\leq j\leq n\}

of
\operatorname{Lag}^{\mathbb{C}}(V)
.
\mathbb{S}_{n}\ltimes H^{n}
acts on
F_{H,\ast}
by

(\sigma,(h_{1},\cdots,h_{n})).\operatorname{span}_{\mathbb{C}}\{\mathbf{w}_{1},\cdots,\mathbf{w}_{n}\}:=\operatorname{span}_{\mathbb{C}}\{h_{\sigma(1)}.\mathbf{w}_{\sigma(1)},\cdots,h_{\sigma(n)}.\mathbf{w}_{\sigma(n)}\}

and the subgroup
H^{n}
acts transitively on
F_{H,\ast}
.
If
H=\langle\sqrt{\mathbf{i}}\rangle
,
H^{n}
acts on
F_{\langle\sqrt{\mathbf{i}}\rangle,\ast}
with the subgroup
\langle-1_{\operatorname{SU}(2)}\rangle^{n}
acting trivially. From (
27
) and Theorem
2.10
,
F_{\langle\sqrt{\mathbf{i}}\rangle,\ast}\cap\operatorname{Lag}^{\mathbb{C}}(\vec{n};V)
is nonempty for every
\vec{n}
. So the decomposition into orbits can be expressed in a notation suggestive of the Bruhat decomposition

\operatorname{Lag}^{\mathbb{C}}((\mathbb{R}^{2})^{n})=\bigcup_{\vec{n}}\operatorname{Lag}^{\mathbb{C}}(\vec{n})=\bigcup_{w\in\mathbb{S}_{n}\ltimes(\langle\sqrt{\mathbf{i}}\rangle/\langle-1\rangle)^{n}}Gw\operatorname{Sp}_{L_{\ast}}((\mathbb{R}^{2})^{n}).L_{\ast}^{\mathbb{C}}.

Remark 2.23

(Partial Cayley transforms and basepoints)
.

Choosing a root space decomposition of
\mathfrak{g}
from a vectorial Cartan subalgebra is equivalent to choosing a Darboux basis up to choice of
\mathbf{e}
s and
\mathbf{f}
s within the splitting
V\cong\mathbb{R}^{\oplus 2n}
(Section
A.1
).

In the case of interest, the partial Cayley transforms

c_{\Gamma}c_{\Sigma}^{2}
of
[
Wol72
]
,
[
Wol69
]
are equal (cf. Equation (
82
), (
83
)) to

h_{\Gamma,\Sigma}=(h_{1},\cdots,h_{n})
where

h_{j}=\begin{cases}\sqrt{\mathbf{i}}&\text{ if }2i\varepsilon_{j}\in\Gamma\\
\mathbf{i}&\text{ if }2i\varepsilon_{j}\in\Sigma.\end{cases}

When
\Gamma
,
\Sigma
are given by (
80
) (
81
), the complex Lagrangian subspace

\mathbf{F}_{\vec{n}}:=h_{\Gamma,\Sigma}.\operatorname{span}_{\mathbb{C}}\{\mathbf{e}_{1}+i\mathbf{f}_{1},\cdots,\mathbf{e}_{n}+i\mathbf{f}_{n}\}

is equal to (in the notation of (
75
))

\left[\begin{pmatrix}1_{n_{0}}&0&0\\
0&1_{n_{+}}&0\\
0&0&1_{n_{-}}\\
0&0&0\\
0&-i1_{n_{+}}&0\\
0&0&i1_{n_{-}}\end{pmatrix}\right]

and
is contained in
F_{\langle\sqrt{\mathbf{i}}\rangle,\ast}\cap\operatorname{Lag}^{\mathbb{C}}(\vec{n})
.

Conjugation by elements of
\mathbb{S}_{n}\ltimes\langle\mathbf{i}\rangle^{n}
stabilizes the maximal torus
\operatorname{SO}(2)^{n}
of the maximal compact subgroup of elements commuting with the compatible linear complex structure
\operatorname{diag}(\mathbf{j},\cdots,\mathbf{j})
in
\operatorname{Sp}((\mathbb{R}^{2})^{n})
, so the group
\mathbb{S}_{n}\ltimes(\langle\sqrt{\mathbf{i}}\rangle/\langle-1\rangle)^{n}
can be viewed as an extension of the Weyl group
\mathbb{S}_{n}\ltimes(\langle\mathbf{i}\rangle/\langle-1\rangle)^{n}
of
\operatorname{Sp}((\mathbb{R}^{2})^{n})
. Taking
H=BO_{48}=\langle\sqrt{\mathbf{i}},\mathbf{j}\rangle
, we observe that this discrete group can be extended further to involve quaternionic symmetries and “square roots of quaternionic symmetries.”

\operatorname{Lag}^{\mathbb{C}}(V)=\bigcup_{w\in\mathbb{S}_{n}\ltimes(O_{24})^{n}}Gw.L_{\ast}^{\mathbb{C}}.

3
Orbit Fibrations

In this section we review the Borel embedding and describe three simultaneous linear symplectic reductions as fiber bundles. The fiber bundles become isomorphic when the total space is restricted to spaces that behave well with respect to a compatible linear complex structure. We show how, in the reduced space, the relevant stabilizer subgroups arise from a choice of involution commuting with a choice of compatible linear complex structure in the reduced linear spaces. We also discuss symmetric space structures and complex structures on the fibers.

3.1
Review of the Borel embedding

In order to discuss particular complex structures we will be using and set up some notation, we will briefly recall the Borel embedding. For matrix forms of the objects appearing in this section, we refer to Section
A.3
.

Denote
G:=\operatorname{Sp}(V)
and
G^{\mathbb{C}}:=\operatorname{Sp}(V^{\mathbb{C}})
. Let
\mathbf{J}
be an
\omega
-compatible linear complex structure on
V
. Denote again by
\mathbf{J}
its complex linear extension to
V^{\mathbb{C}}
.

Since we assume the linear symplectic group comes with its fundamental representation, we assume
G\subseteq\operatorname{End}(V)
, and we can view
\mathbf{J}
as an element of
G=\operatorname{Sp}(V)
. Then
\operatorname{Ad}(\mathbf{J})
is a Cartan involution of
G
. In fact, the following holds:

Proposition 3.1
.

There is a bijection between the Cartan involutions on
\mathfrak{g}
and the choice of
\omega
-compatible almost complex structures
\mathbf{J}
on
V
.

Proof.

Take a Darboux basis
\{\mathbf{e},\mathbf{J}\mathbf{e}\}
of
V
. With this basis, we identify Lie algebra of
G
as

\mathfrak{g}=\left\{\mathbf{X}=\begin{pmatrix}\mathbf{a}&\mathbf{b}\\
\mathbf{c}&-\mathbf{a}^{t}\end{pmatrix}:\mathbf{b},\mathbf{c}\in\operatorname{Mat}_{n\times n}(\mathbb{R})^{t},\mathbf{a}\in\operatorname{Mat}_{n\times n}(\mathbb{R})\right\}.

We can compute

\operatorname{Ad}(\mathbf{J})\mathbf{X}:=\begin{pmatrix}0&-1_{n}\\
1_{n}&0\end{pmatrix}\begin{pmatrix}\mathbf{a}&\mathbf{b}\\
\mathbf{c}&-\mathbf{a}^{t}\end{pmatrix}\begin{pmatrix}0&1_{n}\\
-1_{n}&0\end{pmatrix}=\begin{pmatrix}-\mathbf{a}^{t}&-\mathbf{c}\\
-\mathbf{b}&\mathbf{a}\end{pmatrix}=-\mathbf{X}^{t}

for all
\mathbf{X}\in\mathfrak{g}
. So
\operatorname{Ad}(\mathbf{J})
is equal to the Cartan involution
\theta(\mathbf{X}):=-\mathbf{X}^{t}
on
\mathfrak{g}
. It is well known that all Cartan involutions are conjugate, and all
\omega
-compatible linear complex structures are conjugate. The conjugates correspond to each other because

\displaystyle\operatorname{Ad}(g\mathbf{J}g^{-1})(\mathbf{X})

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle(g\mathbf{J}g^{-1})\mathbf{X}(g\mathbf{J}g^{-1})

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle-g(g^{-1}\mathbf{X}g)^{t}g^{-1}=(\operatorname{Ad}(g)\circ\theta\circ\operatorname{Ad}(g^{-1}))(\mathbf{X}).

g
commutes with
\mathbf{J}
if and only if
\operatorname{Ad}(g)
commutes with
\theta
, so both spaces can be parametrized by
G/K
.
∎

Let
K^{\mathbb{C}}\leq G^{\mathbb{C}}
, and
K\leq G
be the subgroups of symplectic linear transformations that commute with
\mathbf{J}
, and
G_{u}\leq G^{\mathbb{C}}
be the subgroup of symplectic linear transformations
g
such that
g\mathbf{J}=\mathbf{J}\overline{g}
. Equivalently,
K^{\mathbb{C}}
,
K
, and
G_{u}
consists of the symplectic linear transformations that preserve, respectively, the bilinear forms
\omega^{\mathbb{C}}(\cdot,\mathbf{J}\cdot)
,
\omega(\cdot,\mathbf{J}\cdot)
, and
\omega^{\mathbb{C}}(\cdot,\mathbf{J}\overline{\cdot})
. Let
\mathfrak{g}
,
\mathfrak{g}^{\mathbb{C}}
,
\mathfrak{g}_{u}
,
\mathfrak{k}^{\mathbb{C}}
,
\mathfrak{k}
denote the corresponding Lie algebras. The decomposition into the
\pm 1
-eigenspaces of
\operatorname{Ad}(\mathbf{J})
,
\mathfrak{g}=\mathfrak{k}\oplus\mathfrak{p}
is a Cartan decomposition of
\mathfrak{g}
.

For all nonzero
\mathbf{v},\mathbf{w}\in V

\omega^{\mathbb{C}}((1_{V^{\mathbb{C}}}\mp i\mathbf{J})\mathbf{v},(1_{V^{\mathbb{C}}}\mp i\mathbf{J})\mathbf{w})=0

and

\pm\kappa((1_{V^{\mathbb{C}}}\mp i\mathbf{J})\mathbf{v},(1_{V^{\mathbb{C}}}\mp i\mathbf{J})\mathbf{v})>0

so
(1_{V^{\mathbb{C}}}-i\mathbf{J})V\in\operatorname{Lag}^{\mathbb{C}}(0,n,0)
. We can also immediately see

\mathbf{F}_{\pm\mathbf{J}}:=(1_{V^{\mathbb{C}}}\mp i\mathbf{J})V

(31)

is the
\pm i
-eigenspace of
\mathbf{J}
.
Denote the projections to
\mathbf{F}_{\pm\mathbf{J}}
along
\mathbf{F}_{\mp\mathbf{J}}
by

\operatorname{pr}_{\pm\mathbf{J}}:=\frac{1}{\sqrt{2}}(1_{V^{\mathbb{C}}}\mp i\mathbf{J})

(32)

Let
P^{\mp}\leq G^{\mathbb{C}}
be the abelian subgroup of symplectic linear transformations (cf. Section
A.3
) that fix all the vectors of
\mathbf{F}_{\mp\mathbf{J}}
. The following is a well known fact.

Lemma 3.2
.

K^{\mathbb{C}}P^{-}
is the stabilizer of
\mathbf{F}_{-\mathbf{J}}
in
G^{\mathbb{C}}
. Moreover,

K=G\cap K^{\mathbb{C}}P^{-}=G_{u}\cap K^{\mathbb{C}}P^{-}.

Proof.

P^{-}
is by definition, contained in the stabilizer of
\mathbf{F}_{-\mathbf{J}}
. Since
\mathbf{F}_{\mathbf{J}}
is transverse to
V
,

\mathbf{F}_{-\mathbf{J}}=\operatorname{pr}_{-\mathbf{J}}V=\operatorname{pr}_{-\mathbf{J}}V^{\mathbb{C}}.

If
k\in K^{\mathbb{C}}
, then

k.\mathbf{F}_{-\mathbf{J}}=k(1_{V^{\mathbb{C}}}+i\mathbf{J})V^{\mathbb{C}}=(1_{V^{\mathbb{C}}}+i\mathbf{J})kV^{\mathbb{C}}=\mathbf{F}_{-\mathbf{J}}.

So
K^{\mathbb{C}}P^{-}
is contained in the stabilizer of
\mathbf{F}_{-\mathbf{J}}
.

If
g\in\operatorname{Sp}(V^{\mathbb{C}})
is in the stabilizer of
\mathbf{F}_{-\mathbf{J}}\in\operatorname{Lag}^{\mathbb{C}}(V)
, then with respect to the splitting
V^{\mathbb{C}}\cong\mathbf{F}_{-\mathbf{J}}\oplus\mathbf{F}_{\mathbf{J}}
, we can write
g
as

g=\begin{pmatrix}g_{11}&g_{12}\\
0&g_{22}\end{pmatrix}=\begin{pmatrix}g_{11}&0\\
0&g_{22}\end{pmatrix}\begin{pmatrix}1_{\mathbf{F}_{-\mathbf{J}}}&g_{11}^{-1}g_{12}\\
0&1_{\mathbf{F}_{\mathbf{J}}}\end{pmatrix}

such that
g_{11}:\mathbf{F}_{-\mathbf{J}}\to\mathbf{F}_{-\mathbf{J}}
,
g_{12}:\mathbf{F}_{\mathbf{J}}\to\mathbf{F}_{-\mathbf{J}}
, and
g_{22}:\mathbf{F}_{\mathbf{J}}\to\mathbf{F}_{\mathbf{J}}
are linear maps.

Then

\begin{pmatrix}1_{-\mathbf{F}_{\mathbf{J}}}&g_{11}^{-1}g_{12}\\
0&1_{\mathbf{F}_{\mathbf{J}}}\end{pmatrix}\in P^{-}.

Moreover, for any
\mathbf{v}\in V^{\mathbb{C}}
there exist
\mathbf{v}_{-\mathbf{J}}\in\mathbf{F}_{-\mathbf{J}}
and
\mathbf{v}_{\mathbf{J}}\in\mathbf{F}_{\mathbf{J}}
such that
\mathbf{v}=\mathbf{v}_{-\mathbf{J}}+\mathbf{v}_{\mathbf{J}}
. Then

\mathbf{J}(g_{11}\mathbf{v}_{-\mathbf{J}}+g_{22}\mathbf{v}_{\mathbf{J}})=-ig_{11}\mathbf{v}_{-\mathbf{J}}+ig_{22}\mathbf{v}_{\mathbf{J}}=g_{11}\mathbf{J}\mathbf{v}_{-\mathbf{J}}+g_{22}\mathbf{J}\mathbf{v}_{\mathbf{J}}

so

\mathbf{J}\begin{pmatrix}g_{11}&0\\
0&g_{22}\end{pmatrix}=\begin{pmatrix}g_{11}&0\\
0&g_{22}\end{pmatrix}\mathbf{J}\implies\begin{pmatrix}g_{11}&0\\
0&g_{22}\end{pmatrix}\in K^{\mathbb{C}}.

Thus the stabilizer of
\mathbf{F}_{-\mathbf{J}}
is equal to
K^{\mathbb{C}}P^{-}
.

If
g\in G\cap K^{\mathbb{C}}P^{-}
,
g=\overline{g}
. Since
\mathbf{F}_{\mathbf{J}}=\overline{\mathbf{F}_{-\mathbf{J}}}
,
g=\overline{g}
implies
g_{12}=\overline{g_{21}}=0
, so that
G\cap P^{-}
is trivial. So
G\cap K^{\mathbb{C}}P^{-}=G\cap K^{\mathbb{C}}=K
.

Since
\mathbf{F}_{-\mathbf{J}}
and
\mathbf{F}_{\mathbf{J}}
are orthogonal with respect to
\omega^{\mathbb{C}}(\cdot,\mathbf{J}\overline{\cdot})
,
G_{u}\cap P^{-}
is trivial. Then
G_{u}\cap K^{\mathbb{C}}P^{-}=G_{u}\cap K^{\mathbb{C}}
consists of those
g\in G^{\mathbb{C}}
such that
\mathbf{J}g=g\mathbf{J}=\mathbf{J}\overline{g}
, so
G_{u}\cap K^{\mathbb{C}}=G\cap K^{\mathbb{C}}=K
.
∎

If we take a basis
\{\mathbf{v}_{1},\cdots,\mathbf{v}_{n}\}
of a complex Lagrangian subspace
\mathbf{F}
that is unitary with respect to
\omega^{\mathbb{C}}(\cdot,\mathbf{J}\overline{\cdot})|_{\mathbf{F}\times\mathbf{F}}
, then
\{\mathbf{v}_{1},\cdots,\mathbf{v}_{n},\mathbf{J}\overline{\mathbf{v}}_{1},\cdots,\mathbf{J}\overline{\mathbf{v}}_{n}\}
is a complex Darboux basis of
V^{\mathbb{C}}
that is unitary with respect to
\omega^{\mathbb{C}}(\cdot,\mathbf{J}\overline{\cdot})
. Thus
G_{u}
acts transitively on
\operatorname{Lag}^{\mathbb{C}}(V)
.

So as a consequence of Lemma
3.2
, the maps

gK\mapsto g.\mathbf{F}_{-\mathbf{J}}
, and
gK^{\mathbb{C}}P^{-}\mapsto g.\mathbf{F}_{-\mathbf{J}}
provide diffeomorphisms

\displaystyle G/K

\displaystyle\xrightarrow{\cong}

\displaystyle\operatorname{Lag}^{\mathbb{C}}(0,0,n;V)

\displaystyle G_{u}/K

\displaystyle\xrightarrow{\cong}

\displaystyle\operatorname{Lag}^{\mathbb{C}}(V)

(33)

\displaystyle G^{\mathbb{C}}/K^{\mathbb{C}}P^{-}

\displaystyle\xrightarrow{\cong}

\displaystyle\operatorname{Lag}^{\mathbb{C}}(V).

So in our case of discussion, the Borel embedding (Proposition 7 of
[
Bor54
]
, and see Proposition I.5.24 of
[
BJ06
]
, Proposition 7.14 of
[
Hel01
]
for expositions)

G/K\hookrightarrow G^{\mathbb{C}}/K^{\mathbb{C}}P^{-}\cong G_{u}/K

(34)

can be expressed as just the
G
-equivariant inclusion

\operatorname{Lag}^{\mathbb{C}}(0,0,n;V)\hookrightarrow\operatorname{Lag}^{\mathbb{C}}(V)

(35)

Remark 3.3

(Dependence on choice of
\mathbf{J}
)
.

The Borel embedding (
34
) exists once
\mathbf{J}
is chosen and depends equivariantly on the choice of
\mathbf{J}
. This is because all Cartan involutions are conjugate, and a choice of maximal compact subgroup
K\leq G
is equivalent to a choice of
\omega
-compatible linear complex structure
\mathbf{J}
. Moreover, since
K^{\mathbb{C}}P^{-}
is a parabolic subgroup of
G^{\mathbb{C}}
, it is its own normalizer. Conjugating by elements of
G
(and
G_{u}
) preserves all the structures in the embedding–i.e. all the statements above hold if we replace
\mathbf{J}
’s by
g\mathbf{J}g^{-1}
’s. However, we note that after the identifications (
33
),(
35
)
does not require making a choice of
\mathbf{J}
at all
and only uses complex conjugation on
V^{\mathbb{C}}
.

Remark 3.4

(Complex structure on
\operatorname{Lag}^{\mathbb{C}}(V)
)
.

If we identify
V^{\mathbb{C}}\cong\mathbb{C}^{2n}
,
G^{\mathbb{C}}
is a linear algebraic group. Since
K^{\mathbb{C}}P^{-}
is a parabolic subgroup of
G^{\mathbb{C}}
,
\operatorname{Lag}^{\mathbb{C}}(V)\cong G_{u}/K\cong G^{\mathbb{C}}/K^{\mathbb{C}}P^{-}
is a (complex) projective variety, and inherits a Kähler structure from complex projective space. In this paper, to make transparent the dependence of the choice of
\omega
-compatible linear complex structure
\mathbf{J}
, we use the following construction of the Kähler structure on
\operatorname{Lag}^{\mathbb{C}}(V)
from
[
Hel01
]
:

Given an
\omega
-compatible linear complex structure
\mathbf{J}
on
V
, let
G_{u}
be the subgroup of
G^{\mathbb{C}}
consisting of the elements fixed by
g\mapsto(g^{\dagger})^{-1}
where
(\cdot)^{\dagger}
is the adjoint with respect to the hermitian inner product
\omega^{\mathbb{C}}(\cdot\mathbf{J}\overline{\cdot})
. Let
K
be fixed locus of the involution
g\mapsto\overline{g}
on
G_{u}
. The image
\operatorname{Ad}(K)\subseteq\operatorname{GL}(\mathfrak{g}_{u})
is compact, so
(G_{u},K)
is a Riemannian symmetric pair. Let
o:=K
be a basepoint of the left coset space
G_{u}/K
. A choice of
K
-invariant bilinear form on
T_{o}(G_{u}/K)\cong\mathfrak{g}_{u}/\mathfrak{k}\cong i\mathfrak{p}
, extends to a
G_{u}
-invariant Riemannian metric on
G_{u}/K
, making it a globally Riemannian symmetric space (Proposition IV.3.4 of
[
Hel01
]
).

We can choose the
K
-invariant bilinear form on
T_{o}(G_{u}/K)
to be the restriction of the Killing form to
i\mathfrak{p}
.

Moreover,

\omega(\mathbf{u},\mathbf{J}\mathbf{v})+\omega(\mathbf{J}\mathbf{u},\mathbf{v})=0\quad\mathbf{u},\mathbf{v}\in V,

so
\mathbf{J}
can be viewed as an element of
\mathfrak{k}\subseteq\operatorname{End}(V)
. Then
\operatorname{Ad}(\mathbf{J})\mathbf{X}=-\mathbf{X}
is equivalent to

\frac{1}{2}\operatorname{ad}(\mathbf{J})\mathbf{X}=\mathbf{J}\mathbf{X}\quad\mathbf{X}\in\mathfrak{p}.

(36)

So
\frac{1}{2}\operatorname{ad}(\mathbf{J})
is a linear complex structure on
i\mathfrak{p}
, compatible with the restriction of the Killing form of
\mathfrak{g}_{u}
to
i\mathfrak{p}
. By Proposition VIII.4.2 in
[
Hel01
]
,
\frac{1}{2}\operatorname{ad}(\mathbf{J})
extends to a unique
G_{u}
-invariant genuine complex structure on
G_{u}/K
, making
\operatorname{Lag}^{\mathbb{C}}(V)
into a hermitian symmetric space of the compact type CI in the notation of
[
Hel01
]
.

3.2
Simultaneous linear symplectic reduction

Definition 3.5

(Normalizers and centralizers of fibers)
.

Suppose
E\xrightarrow{\pi}B
is a smooth surjective map, and a real Lie group
G
acts on
E
smoothly. Let
N(\pi^{-1}(b)):=\{g\in G:g.\pi^{-1}(b)=\pi^{-1}(b)\}
and
C(\pi^{-1}(b)):=\{g\in G:g|_{\pi^{-1}(b)}=1_{\pi^{-1}(b)}\}
. We will denote by
N(b):=N(\pi^{-1}(b))
and
C(b):=C(\pi^{-1}(b))
when
\pi
is clear from context. If
\pi(x)=b
, then denote the isotropy subgroup of
x
in
N(\pi^{-1}(b))
by
N(\pi^{-1}(b))_{x}
(or simply by
N(b)_{x}
).

Remark 3.6
.

C(b)
is the kernel of the action of
N(b)
on
\pi^{-1}(b)
, and hence a normal subgroup of
N(b)
.
C(b)
is also a subgroup of
N(b)_{x}
, so it is also a normal subgroup of
N(b)_{x}
for every
x\in\pi^{-1}(b)
.

The following statement may be well known.

Lemma 3.7
.

Suppose
M
is a smooth manifold, and
G
is a real Lie group acting smoothly, freely, and properly on
M
from the right. Suppose
H\leq G
is a closed subgroup. Then the orbit space
M/H
is a smooth manifold and there is a smooth fibration of orbit spaces

\pi:M/H\to M/G\quad xH\mapsto xG\quad x\in M

with diffeomorphism of fibers

G/H\xrightarrow{\cong}\pi^{-1}(xG)\quad gH\mapsto xgH.

Proof.

H
acts on
M
smoothly and freely. Since
H
is closed, the inclusion map to
G
is proper, and
H
acts on
M
properly. So
M/H
is a smooth manifold such that the quotient map
M\twoheadrightarrow M/H
is a smooth surjective submersion.
M\twoheadrightarrow M/G
is constant on the fibers of
M\twoheadrightarrow M/H
, so we can pass smoothly to the quotient to obtain
\pi:M/H\twoheadrightarrow M/G
.
∎

The following statement may be well known.

Lemma 3.8
.

We assume the setup of Definition
3.5
.

1.

If
b\in B
is a regular value of
\pi
,
N(b)
acts transitively on
\pi^{-1}(b)
, and suppose
N\leq C(b)
is a normal subgroup of both
N(b)
and
N(b)_{x}
. Then there is an
N(b)\twoheadrightarrow N(b)/N
-equivariant diffeomorphism

\pi^{-1}(b)\cong N(b)/N(b)_{x}\cong(N(b)/N)/(N(b)_{x}/N).

2.

If
G
acts on
B
and
\pi
is
G
-equivariant, then
g\in G
is an element of
N(b)
if and only if there exist
x,x^{\prime}\in\pi^{-1}(b)
such that
g.x=x^{\prime}
. Moreover,
N(b)
is equal to
G_{b}
, the isotropy subgroup of
b
in
G
, and
N(b)_{x}
is equal to
G_{x}
.

3.

If
G
acts on
B
,
\pi
is
G
-equivariant, and
G
acts on
E
transitively, then

(a)

G
acts transitively on
B
, and
N(b)
acts transitively on
\pi^{-1}(b)
for every
b\in B
.

(b)

For every
b,b^{\prime}\in B
,
N(b)
is conjugate to
N(b^{\prime})
. If
\pi(x)=b
and
\pi(x^{\prime})=b^{\prime}
, then
N(b)_{x}
is conjugate to
N(b^{\prime})_{x^{\prime}}
.

(c)

\pi
is a fiber bundle. Moreover, for every
(x,b)
such that
\pi(x)=b
, there are
G
-equivariant diffeomorphisms such that the following diagram commutes:

\hbox to243.96pt{\vbox to56.24pt{\pgfpicture\makeatletter\hbox{\hskip 121.98044pt\lower-27.0472pt\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin=1} \lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke=#000000} \lxSVG@begingroup@{fill=#000000} \lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width=0.4pt} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\nullfont\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin=1} {}{}{}{{}}\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\hbox{\hbox{{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin=1} {{}{}{{{}}{{}}{{}}{{}}{{}}{{}}}{{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}}{{}{}{{

}{{{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin=1} \lxSVG@transformcm{1.0}{0.0}{0.0}{1.0}{-43.25586pt}{-2.8978pt}\lxSVG@begingroup@{transform=matrix(1.0 0.0 0.0 1.0 -59.85 -4.01)} \pgfsys@hbox{58}\lxSVG@closescope }}}

\lxSVG@closescope
\lxSVG@closescope {\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}\hss}\lxSVG@discardpath\lxSVG@closescope \hss}}\lxSVG@closescope\endpgfpicture}}.

Here
\pi^{\prime}
is as in Lemma
3.7
and
\pi^{\prime\prime}
is an associated bundle to the principal
N(b)
-bundle
G\twoheadrightarrow G/N(b)
.
G/G_{x}
and
G/G_{b}
are viewed as both left coset spaces and orbit spaces.

Proof.

(1):
\pi^{-1}(b)
is closed submanifold of
E
, so
N(b)
is closed.
N(b)
is also a subgroup of
G
, and hence a Lie subgroup (Theorem II.2.3 of
[
Hel01
]
). By assumption
\pi^{-1}(b)
is a homogeneous space. The diffeomorphism and equivariance are well known (see for instance Theorem II.3.2, Proposition II.4.3 of
[
Hel01
]
). (2): If there exist
x,x^{\prime}\in\pi^{-1}(b)
such that
g.x=x^{\prime}
,
g.b=g.\pi(x)=\pi(g.x)=\pi(x^{\prime})=b
, so
g\in G_{b}
. If
g\in G_{b}
, for any
y\in\pi^{-1}(b)
,
\pi(g.y)=g.\pi(y)=g.b=b
. So
g\in N(b)
. If
g\in N(b)
, for any
x\in\pi^{-1}(b)
,
g.x\in\pi^{-1}(b)
.
N(b)_{x}\leq G_{x}
by definition.
G_{x}\leq N(b)_{x}
by what we have just shown. (3a): If
b,b^{\prime}\in B
, take any
x\in\pi^{-1}(b)
and
x^{\prime}\in\pi^{-1}(b)
. By assumption, there exists
g\in G
such that
g.x=x^{\prime}
. Then since
\pi
is
G
-equivariant,
g.b=g.\pi(x)=\pi(g.x)=\pi(x^{\prime})=b^{\prime}
. For any
x,x^{\prime}\in\pi^{-1}(b)
, there exists
g\in G
such that
g.x=x^{\prime}
. By (2),
g\in N(b)
. (3b): From (2)
G_{x}=N(b)_{x}
,
G_{x}^{\prime}=N(b^{\prime})_{x^{\prime}}
, so it follows from transitivity that the isotropy subgroups are all conjugate. Similarly, from (2)
G_{b}=N(b)
,
G_{b^{\prime}}=N(b^{\prime})
, and they are conjugate as isotropy subgroups. (3c): If
x^{\prime}\in E
, by the transitivity assumption, there exists an
h\in G
such that
hx^{\prime}=x
. Then
x^{\prime}\mapsto hG_{x}
gives a map
E\to G/G_{x}
not depending on the choice of
h
, that is well known to be a diffeomorphism. If
h^{\prime}\in G
such that
h^{\prime}.\pi(x^{\prime})=b
, then
h^{\prime}.x^{\prime}\in\pi^{-1}(b)
, so there exists a
g\in N(b)=G_{b}
such that
gh^{\prime}=h
by (3a). So
h^{\prime}G_{b}=hg^{-1}G_{b}=hG_{b}
, and the left square commutes. The right square commutes by Proposition I.5.5 of
[
KN63
]
.
∎

(Simultaneous linear symplectic reduction)

(37)

1.

\alpha
,
\varphi
,
(\cdot\cap\cdot^{\omega})
,
\operatorname{Re}\cdot_{\geq 0}
,
\operatorname{Re}\cdot_{0}
are fiber bundles.

2.

If
\pi=\alpha,\operatorname{Re}\cdot_{0},(\cdot\cap\cdot^{\omega})
and
b=W_{0}\in\operatorname{Gr}(n_{0},0,n-n_{0})
, or if
\pi=\operatorname{Re}\cdot_{\geq 0}
and
b=W\in\operatorname{Gr}(\vec{n})
, or if
\pi=\varphi
and
b=\mathbf{F}\in\operatorname{Lag}^{\mathbb{C}}(\vec{n})
, then the normalizers of the fibers are
N(\pi^{-1}(b))\cong\operatorname{Sp}_{b}(V)
.

3.

If
\pi=\varphi,\alpha,\operatorname{Re}\cdot_{\geq 0}
and
x=\mathbf{F}^{\oplus}\in\operatorname{Lag}^{\mathbb{C}}_{\oplus}(\vec{n})
, or if
\pi=\operatorname{Re}\cdot_{0}
and
x=\mathbf{F}\in\operatorname{Lag}^{\mathbb{C}}(\vec{n})
, or if
\pi=\cdot\cap\cdot^{\omega}
and
x=W^{\prime}\in\operatorname{Gr}(\vec{n})
, then the isotropy subgroups of the action of
N(\pi^{-1}(\pi(x)))
on
\pi^{-1}(\pi(x))
are
N(\pi^{-1}(\pi(x)))_{x}\cong\operatorname{Sp}_{x}(V)
.

4.

For all
W_{0}\in\operatorname{Gr}(n_{0},0,n-n_{0})
there are diffeomorphisms for the fibers

\displaystyle\alpha^{-1}(W_{0})

\displaystyle\xrightarrow{\cong}

\displaystyle\operatorname{Lag}_{\oplus}^{\mathbb{C}}(0,n_{+},n_{-};W_{0}^{\omega}/W_{0})

\displaystyle\mathbf{F}^{\oplus}

\displaystyle\mapsto

\displaystyle\mathbf{F}^{\oplus}/\mathbf{F}_{0}

\displaystyle(\operatorname{Re}\cdot_{0})^{-1}(W_{0})

\displaystyle\xrightarrow{\cong}

\displaystyle\operatorname{Lag}^{\mathbb{C}}(0,n_{+},n_{-};W_{0}^{\omega}/W_{0})

\displaystyle\mathbf{F}

\displaystyle\mapsto

\displaystyle\mathbf{F}/\mathbf{F}_{0}

\displaystyle(\cdot\cap\cdot^{\omega})^{-1}(W_{0})

\displaystyle\xrightarrow{\cong}

\displaystyle\operatorname{Gr}(0,n_{+},n_{-};W_{0}^{\omega}/W_{0})

\displaystyle W^{\prime}

\displaystyle\mapsto

\displaystyle W^{\prime}/W_{0}

equivariant with respect to the isomorphism

\operatorname{Sp}_{W_{0}}(V)/(\operatorname{GL}(W_{0})\ltimes H(W_{0}))\xrightarrow{\cong}\operatorname{Sp}(W_{0}^{\omega}/W_{0}).

(38)

Similarly, for all
W\in\operatorname{Gr}(\vec{n})
there is a diffeomorphism

\displaystyle(\operatorname{Re}\cdot_{\geq 0})^{-1}(W)

\displaystyle\xrightarrow{\cong}

\displaystyle\operatorname{Lag}^{\mathbb{C}}(0,n_{+},0;W/W_{0})\times\operatorname{Lag}^{\mathbb{C}}(0,0,n_{+};W^{\omega}/W_{0})

\displaystyle\mathbf{F}^{\oplus}

\displaystyle\mapsto

\displaystyle(\mathbf{F}^{\oplus}_{\geq 0}/\mathbf{F}_{0},\mathbf{F}^{\oplus}_{\leq 0}/\mathbf{F}_{0})

equivariant with respect to the isomorphism
\operatorname{Sp}_{W}(V)/(\operatorname{GL}(W_{0})\ltimes H(W))\xrightarrow{\cong}\operatorname{Sp}(W/W_{0})\times\operatorname{Sp}(W^{\omega}/W_{0})
.

5.

If
\pi=\alpha,\operatorname{Re}\cdot_{0},(\cdot\cap\cdot^{\omega})
and
b=W_{0}\in\operatorname{Gr}(n_{0},0,n-n_{0})
, or if
\pi=\operatorname{Re}\cdot_{\geq 0}
and
b=W\in\operatorname{Gr}(\vec{n})
, or if
\pi=\varphi
and
b=\mathbf{F}\in\operatorname{Lag}^{\mathbb{C}}(\vec{n})
, then there are short exact sequences of groups:

\operatorname{GL}(\operatorname{Re}b_{0})\ltimes H(\operatorname{Re}b)\hookrightarrow C(\pi^{-1}(b))\twoheadrightarrow Z(\operatorname{Sp}((\operatorname{Re}b_{0})^{\omega}/\operatorname{Re}b_{0}))

(39)

Z(\operatorname{Sp}(V))\hookrightarrow C(\pi^{-1}(b))\twoheadrightarrow(\operatorname{GL}(\operatorname{Re}b_{0})/\{\pm 1_{\operatorname{GL}(\operatorname{Re}b_{0})}\})\ltimes H(\operatorname{Re}b).

(40)

Proof.

(1): By Theorems
2.2
,
2.10
,
\operatorname{Sp}(V)
acts transitively on all the spaces in the diagram (
37
), and all the maps in the diagram (
37
) are
\operatorname{Sp}(V)
-equivariant. So Lemma
3.8
.3c, all the maps in the diagram (
37
) are a fiber bundles. (2), (3): The computation of
N(\pi^{-1}(b))
and
N(\pi^{-1}(\pi(x)))_{x}
follows from Lemma
3.8
.2, and the definitions of the relevant groups. (4): This follows from Lemma
3.8
.1, where we take
N
to be
\operatorname{GL}(W_{0})\ltimes H(W)
. (5): Suppose
\pi=\alpha,\operatorname{Re}\cdot_{0},(\cdot\cap\cdot^{\omega})
and
b=W_{0}\in\operatorname{Gr}(n_{0},0,n-n_{0})
. Under the isomorphism (
38
),
C(\pi^{-1}(b))
gets mapped to the the kernel of the action of
\operatorname{Sp}(b^{\omega}/b)
, which is
Z(\operatorname{Sp}(b^{\omega}/b))
. Since
Z(\operatorname{Sp}(V))
is contained in
C(\pi^{-1}(b))
, this map is surjective, giving us (
39
), as
b=\operatorname{Re}b_{0}
. So
C(\pi^{-1}(b))
is generated by
Z(\operatorname{Sp}(V))
and
\operatorname{GL}(b)\ltimes H(b)
. Take a Darboux basis
\{\mathbf{e},\mathbf{f}\}
given by Theorem
2.2
associated to
b
. Then writing out the elements in their matrix forms, we can check the short exact sequence in (
40
). For
\pi=\operatorname{Re}\cdot_{\geq 0}
,
b=W\in\operatorname{Gr}(\vec{n})
, we can prove it in the same way. If
\pi=\varphi
and
b=\mathbf{F}\in\operatorname{Lag}^{\mathbb{C}}(\vec{n})
,
\operatorname{U}(\mathbf{F}/\mathbf{F}_{0})
acts transitively on
\varphi^{-1}(\mathbf{F})
via the identification

\operatorname{Sp}_{b}(V)/(\operatorname{GL}(\operatorname{Re}b_{0})\ltimes H(\operatorname{Re}b_{0}))\xrightarrow{\cong}\operatorname{U}(\mathbf{F}/\mathbf{F}_{0})\leq\operatorname{Sp}((\operatorname{Re}b_{0})^{\omega}/\operatorname{Re}b_{0}).

(41)

If we take a Darboux basis
\{\mathbf{e},\mathbf{f}\}
associated to
\mathbf{F}
given by Theorem
2.10
, we can inspect that the kernel of this action is
Z(\operatorname{Sp}((\operatorname{Re}b_{0})^{\omega}/\operatorname{Re}b_{0}))
, and that under the isomorphism in (
41
),
C(\pi^{-1}(b))
gets mapped to the kernel
Z(\operatorname{Sp}((\operatorname{Re}b_{0})^{\omega}/\operatorname{Re}b_{0}))
. The rest of the proof proceeds as above.
∎

Remark 3.10

(Extending actions on the fibers)
.

The group of linear symplectomorphisms of the reduced space
\operatorname{Sp}(W_{0}^{\omega}/W_{0})
acts on
\operatorname{Lag}_{\oplus}^{\mathbb{C}}(0,n_{+},n_{-};W_{0}^{\omega}/W_{0})
,
\operatorname{Lag}^{\mathbb{C}}(0,n_{+},n_{-};W_{0}^{\omega}/W_{0})
, and
\operatorname{Gr}(0,n_{+},n_{-};W_{0}^{\omega}/W_{0})
. For any choice of complementary subspace
W_{-}
of
W_{0}
in
W_{0}^{\omega}
, this action identifies with an action of
\operatorname{Sp}(W_{-})
on
\alpha^{-1}(W_{0})
,
(\operatorname{Re}\cdot_{0})^{-1}(W_{0})
,
(\cdot\cap\cdot^{\omega})^{-1}(W_{0})
via
\operatorname{Sp}(W_{0}^{\omega}/W_{0})\cong\operatorname{Sp}(W_{-})
, and further extends to an action on
\operatorname{Lag}_{\oplus}^{\mathbb{C}}(\vec{n})
,
\operatorname{Lag}^{\mathbb{C}}(\vec{n})
,
\operatorname{Gr}(\vec{n})
via the splitting homomorphism in Theorem
2.9
:

\operatorname{Sp}(W_{0}^{\omega}/W_{0})\cong\operatorname{Sp}(W_{-})\hookrightarrow\operatorname{Sp}_{W_{0}^{\omega}}(V)\leq\operatorname{Sp}(V).

Remark 3.11

(Boundary components and compatible complex structures)
.

For an
\omega
-compatible linear complex structure
\mathbf{J}
, consider the map
\mathbf{J}\mapsto\mathbf{F}_{\mathbf{J}}=(1_{V^{\mathbb{C}}}-i\mathbf{J})V\in\operatorname{Lag}^{\mathbb{C}}(0,n,0)
. This map is a diffeomorphism from the set of
\omega
-compatible linear complex structures to
\operatorname{Lag}^{\mathbb{C}}(0,n,0)
. So
when
n_{-}=0
, the fibers
\alpha^{-1}(W)
can be identified with the set of
\tilde{\omega}
-compatible linear complex structures on
W^{\omega}/W
. They are referred to as
boundary components
in the literature, for instance,
[
WK65a
]
,
[
BJ06
]
,
[
BB66
]
.

We can also state a version of Theorem
3.9
, when we are given an
\omega
-compatible linear complex structure
\mathbf{J}
on
V
. In this situation, the fibers of
\operatorname{Re}\cdot_{\geq 0}
and
\varphi
reduce to points.

Definition 3.12

(Orbits of maximal compact subgroups)
.

Suppose
\mathbf{J}
is an
\omega
-compatible linear complex structure on
V
.

1.

Let
\operatorname{Gr}_{\mathbf{J}}(\vec{n})\subseteq\operatorname{Gr}(\vec{n})
be the submanifold consisting of
W\subseteq V
of type
\vec{n}
such that
W\oplus\mathbf{J}W_{0}
is
\mathbf{J}
-invariant.

2.

Let
\operatorname{Lag}^{\mathbb{C}}_{\mathbf{J}}(\vec{n})\subseteq\operatorname{Lag}^{\mathbb{C}}(\vec{n})
be the submanifold consisting of complex Lagrangian subspaces
\mathbf{F}\subseteq V^{\mathbb{C}}
of type
\vec{n}
such that

\mathbf{F}=\mathbf{F}_{0}\oplus(\mathbf{F}\cap\mathbf{F}_{\mathbf{J}})\oplus(\mathbf{F}\cap\mathbf{F}_{-\mathbf{J}}).

3.

Let
\operatorname{Lag}^{\mathbb{C}}_{\mathbf{J},\oplus}(\vec{n})\subseteq\operatorname{Lag}^{\mathbb{C}}_{\oplus}(\vec{n})
be the submanifold consisting of equivalence classes of complex Lagrangian subspaces of type
\vec{n}
with splitting
\mathbf{F}^{\oplus}
such that there exist
\mathbf{F}_{\pm}=\mathbf{F}\cap\mathbf{F}_{\pm\mathbf{J}}
such that
\mathbf{F}^{\oplus}=[(\mathbf{F},\mathbf{F}_{+},\mathbf{F}_{-})]
.

Suppose
W=W_{0}\in\operatorname{Gr}_{\mathbf{J}}(\vec{n};V)
. Let
W_{-}^{\mathbf{J}}
be the
\omega(\cdot,\mathbf{J}\cdot)
-orthogonal complement of
W
in
W^{\omega}
. By Theorem
2.3
, there exists a unique
\mathbf{J}
-invariant subspace
W_{-}^{\mathbf{J}}
of
W^{\omega}
complementary to
W
in
W^{\omega}
. Moreover,
W_{-}^{\mathbf{J}}=(W\oplus\mathbf{J}W)^{\omega}
as
\mathbf{J}
-invariant symplectic subspaces of
V
. Then
\omega
descends to a symplectic form on both
\tilde{W}=W^{\omega}/W
and
V/(W\oplus\mathbf{J}W)
, and we will denote both by
\tilde{\omega}
. Moreover, there exist
\tilde{\omega}
-compatible linear complex structures on both
\tilde{W}=W^{\omega}/W
and
V/(W\oplus\mathbf{J}W)
. We will again denote both by
\tilde{\mathbf{J}}(W)
, so that

(W_{-}^{\mathbf{J}},\omega|_{W_{-}^{\mathbf{J}}},\mathbf{J}|_{W_{-}^{\mathbf{J}}})\cong(W^{\omega}/W,\tilde{\omega},\tilde{\mathbf{J}}(W))\cong(V/(W\oplus\mathbf{J}W),\tilde{\omega},\tilde{\mathbf{J}}(W))

as symplectic vector spaces with compatible linear complex structure. In particular,
\mathbf{J}
descends to every
W^{\omega}/W
simultaneously.

We can observe that

\iota_{\mathbf{J}}|_{\operatorname{Lag}_{\mathbf{J}}^{\mathbb{C}}(\vec{n})}(\mathbf{F}):=[\mathbf{F}_{0}\oplus(\mathbf{F}\cap\mathbf{F}_{\mathbf{J}})\oplus(\mathbf{F}\cap\mathbf{F}_{-\mathbf{J}})]

is a diffeomorphism from
\operatorname{Lag}_{\mathbf{J}}^{\mathbb{C}}(\vec{n})
to
\operatorname{Lag}_{\mathbf{J},\oplus}^{\mathbb{C}}(\vec{n})
with inverse
\varphi|_{\operatorname{Lag}_{\mathbf{J},\oplus}^{\mathbb{C}}(\vec{n})}
.

Similarly,

\psi_{\mathbf{J}}(W):=[W_{0}\oplus(1_{V^{\mathbb{C}}}-i\mathbf{J})W\oplus(1_{V^{\mathbb{C}}}+i\mathbf{J})W^{\omega}]

is a diffeomorphism from
\operatorname{Gr}_{\mathbf{J}}(\vec{n})
to
\operatorname{Lag}_{\mathbf{J},\oplus}^{\mathbb{C}}(\vec{n})
with inverse
\operatorname{Re}\cdot_{\geq 0}|_{\operatorname{Lag}_{\mathbf{J},\oplus}^{\mathbb{C}}(\vec{n})}
.

Proposition 3.13

(Compact orbit fibrations)
.

Suppose
\mathbf{J}
be an
\omega
-compatible linear complex structure on
V
, and
K\leq G=\operatorname{Sp}(V)
is the maximal compact subgroup of linear symplectomorphisms of
V
that commute with
\mathbf{J}
.

1.

K
acts transitively on
\operatorname{Gr}_{\mathbf{J}}(\vec{n})
,
\operatorname{Lag}_{\mathbf{J}}^{\mathbb{C}}(\vec{n})
, and
\operatorname{Lag}_{\mathbf{J},\oplus}^{\mathbb{C}}(\vec{n})
. For each choice of
W\in\operatorname{Gr}_{\mathbf{J}}(\vec{n})
,
\mathbf{F}\in\operatorname{Lag}_{\mathbf{J}}^{\mathbb{C}}(\vec{n})
, and
\mathbf{F}^{\oplus}\in\operatorname{Lag}_{\mathbf{J},\oplus}^{\mathbb{C}}(\vec{n})
there are diffeomorphisms

\displaystyle\operatorname{Gr}_{\mathbf{J}}(\vec{n})

\displaystyle\cong

\displaystyle K/(K\cap\operatorname{Sp}_{W}(V))

\displaystyle\cong

\displaystyle\operatorname{U}(n)/(O(n_{0})\times\operatorname{U}(n_{+})\times\operatorname{U}(n_{-}))

\displaystyle\operatorname{Lag}_{\mathbf{J}}^{\mathbb{C}}(\vec{n})

\displaystyle\cong

\displaystyle K/(K\cap\operatorname{Sp}_{\mathbf{F}}(V))

\displaystyle\cong

\displaystyle\operatorname{U}(n)/(O(n_{0})\times\operatorname{U}(n_{+})\times\operatorname{U}(n_{-}))

\displaystyle\operatorname{Lag}_{\mathbf{J},\oplus}^{\mathbb{C}}(\vec{n})

\displaystyle\cong

\displaystyle K/(K\cap\operatorname{Sp}_{\mathbf{F}^{\oplus}}(V))

\displaystyle\cong

\displaystyle\operatorname{U}(n)/(O(n_{0})\times\operatorname{U}(n_{+})\times\operatorname{U}(n_{-}))

\displaystyle.

2.

The maps

\displaystyle(\cdot\cap\cdot^{\omega})|_{\operatorname{Gr}_{\mathbf{J}}(\vec{n})}:

\displaystyle\operatorname{Gr}_{\mathbf{J}}(\vec{n})

\displaystyle\to

\displaystyle\operatorname{Gr}(n_{0},0,n-n_{0})

\displaystyle(\operatorname{Re}\cdot_{0})|_{\operatorname{Lag}_{\mathbf{J}}^{\mathbb{C}}(\vec{n})}:

\displaystyle\operatorname{Lag}_{\mathbf{J}}^{\mathbb{C}}(\vec{n})

\displaystyle\to

\displaystyle\operatorname{Gr}(n_{0},0,n-n_{0})

\displaystyle\alpha|_{\operatorname{Lag}_{\mathbf{J},\oplus}^{\mathbb{C}}(\vec{n})}:

\displaystyle\operatorname{Lag}_{\mathbf{J},\oplus}^{\mathbb{C}}(\vec{n})

\displaystyle\to

\displaystyle\operatorname{Gr}(n_{0},0,n-n_{0})

are fiber bundles with fibers diffeomorphic to the ordinary complex Grassmannian of complex
n_{+}
dimensional subspaces in complex
n-n_{0}
dimensional space.

Proof.

(1): If
\mathbf{F}^{\oplus}\in\operatorname{Lag}_{\mathbf{J},\oplus}^{\mathbb{C}}(\vec{n})
, take a Darboux basis
\{\mathbf{e},\mathbf{f}\}
given by Theorem
2.10
. Since
\mathbf{e}^{+}_{k}-i\mathbf{f}^{+}_{k}
is in the
+i
-eigenspace of
\mathbf{J}
,
\mathbf{f}^{+}_{k}=\mathbf{J}\mathbf{e}^{+}_{k}
for
1\leq k\leq n_{+}
. Similarly,
\mathbf{f}^{-}_{k}=\mathbf{J}\mathbf{e}^{-}_{k}
for
1\leq k\leq n_{-}
. Then we have constructed a Darboux basis
\{\mathbf{e}^{0},\mathbf{e}^{+},\mathbf{e}^{-},\mathbf{J}\mathbf{e}^{0},\mathbf{J}\mathbf{e}^{+},\mathbf{J}\mathbf{e}^{-}\}
such that
\mathbf{F}_{0}=\operatorname{span}_{\mathbb{C}}\{\mathbf{e}^{0}\}
,
\mathbf{F}_{+}=\operatorname{span}_{\mathbb{C}}\{\mathbf{e}^{+}-i\mathbf{J}\mathbf{e}^{+}\}
, and
\mathbf{F}_{-}=\operatorname{span}_{\mathbb{C}}\{\mathbf{e}^{-}+i\mathbf{J}\mathbf{e}^{-}\}
. Then for any two choices of
\mathbf{F}^{\oplus},(\mathbf{F}^{\oplus})^{\prime}\in\operatorname{Lag}_{\mathbf{J},\oplus}^{\mathbb{C}}(\vec{n})
, the linear extension of the map assigning one such Darboux basis obtained as above to the other is a symplectic linear transformation and commutes with
\mathbf{J}
. So
K
acts transitively on
\operatorname{Lag}_{\mathbf{J},\oplus}^{\mathbb{C}}(\vec{n})
. Since
\operatorname{Re}\cdot_{\geq 0}|_{\operatorname{Lag}_{\mathbf{J},\oplus}^{\mathbb{C}}(\vec{n})}
and
\varphi|_{\operatorname{Lag}_{\mathbf{J},\oplus}^{\mathbb{C}}(\vec{n})}
are
K
-equivariant diffeomorphisms,
K
acts transitively on
\operatorname{Gr}_{\mathbf{J}}(\vec{n})
and
\operatorname{Lag}_{\mathbf{J}}^{\mathbb{C}}(\vec{n})
as well (Lemma
3.8
.3a).
(2):
\alpha|_{\operatorname{Lag}_{\mathbf{J},\oplus}^{\mathbb{C}}(\vec{n})}
,
\operatorname{Re}\cdot_{0}|_{\operatorname{Lag}_{\mathbf{J}}^{\mathbb{C}}(\vec{n})}
, and
(\cdot\cap\cdot^{\omega})|_{\operatorname{Gr}_{\mathbf{J}}(\vec{n})}
are
K
-equivariant. Since
K
acts transitively on the total spaces, we can apply Lemma
3.8
. In particular, these maps are fiber bundles by Lemma
3.8
.3c.

We can also apply Lemma
3.8
.1 to compute the diffeomorphism of the fibers as follows. If
W^{\prime}\in(\cdot\cap\cdot^{\omega})|_{\operatorname{Gr}_{\mathbf{J}}(\vec{n})}^{-1}(W_{0})
, then
W^{\prime}/W_{0}
is a complex subspace of
(W_{0}^{\omega}/W_{0},\tilde{\mathbf{J}}(W_{0}))
. Similarly, if
\mathbf{F}\in(\operatorname{Re}\cdot_{0})|_{\operatorname{Lag}_{\mathbf{J}}^{\mathbb{C}}(\vec{n})}^{-1}(W_{0})
, then
\operatorname{Re}((\mathbf{F}_{0}+\mathbf{F}\cap\mathbf{F}_{\mathbf{J}}))/\operatorname{Re}\mathbf{F}_{0}
is a complex linear subspace of
(W_{0}^{\omega}/W_{0},\tilde{\mathbf{J}}(W_{0}))
, and if
\mathbf{F}^{\oplus}\in\alpha|_{\operatorname{Lag}_{\mathbf{J},\oplus}^{\mathbb{C}}(\vec{n})}
, then
\operatorname{Re}\mathbf{F}^{\oplus}_{\geq 0}
is a complex subspace of
(W_{0}^{\omega}/W_{0},\tilde{\mathbf{J}}(W_{0}))
. These maps to the ordinary complex Grassmannian of complex linear subspaces of
(W_{0}^{\omega}/W_{0},\tilde{\mathbf{J}}(W_{0}))
are equivariant with respect to
N(\pi^{-1}(b))/N=(K\cap\operatorname{Sp}_{b}(V))/(K\cap(\operatorname{GL}(b)\ltimes H(b)))\xrightarrow{\cong}\operatorname{Sp}(b^{\omega}/b)^{\operatorname{Ad}(\tilde{\mathbf{J}}(W_{0}))}
, where
b=W_{0}
, and
\pi=(\cdot\cap\cdot^{\omega})|_{\operatorname{Gr}_{\mathbf{J}}(\vec{n})}
,
\operatorname{Re}\cdot_{0}|_{\operatorname{Lag}_{\mathbf{J}}^{\mathbb{C}}(\vec{n})}
, or
\alpha|_{\operatorname{Lag}_{\mathbf{J},\oplus}^{\mathbb{C}}(\vec{n})}
. Since these groups act transitively, the maps are diffeomorphisms.
∎

Remark 3.14
.

In the isotropic and coisotropic case (
n_{+}n_{-}=0
),
\operatorname{Gr}_{\mathbf{J}}(\vec{n})=\operatorname{Gr}(\vec{n})
and Proposition
3.13
.1 for
\operatorname{Gr}(\vec{n})
was obtained in
[
Arn67
]
for the Lagrangian case and
[
OP05
]
for the coisotropic case. The statement about the fibers
(\operatorname{Re}\cdot_{0}|_{\operatorname{Lag}_{\mathbf{J}}^{\mathbb{C}}(\vec{n})})^{-1}(W_{0})
in Proposition
3.13
.2 appears in Lemma 11.4 of
[
Wol69
]
and Lemma 9.8 of
[
Wol72
]
.

3.3
Involutions and recompositions

Suppose
W=W_{0}
is an isotropic subspace of
V
and denote by
\tilde{\omega}
the descended symplectic form on
\tilde{W}:=W^{\omega}/W
. For convenience in notation we will suppress the dependence on the choice of
W
in the following. Denote
\tilde{G}:=\operatorname{Sp}(\tilde{W})
. Let
\tilde{\mathbf{J}}
be a linear complex structure on
\tilde{W}
compatible with
\tilde{\omega}
. Let
\tilde{I}
be an involution on
\tilde{W}
in
\tilde{G}
such that it commutes with
\tilde{\mathbf{J}}
and its
\pm 1
-eigenspaces have real dimensions
2n_{\pm}
. Since
\tilde{I}
commutes with
\tilde{\mathbf{J}}
, its
\pm 1
-eigenspaces are
\tilde{\mathbf{J}}
-invariant, symplectic, and are symplectic complements (and
\tilde{\omega}(\cdot,\tilde{\mathbf{J}}\cdot)
-orthogonal complements) of each other. Conversely, a splitting of
\tilde{W}
into symplectic,
\tilde{\mathbf{J}}
-invariant subspaces with the appropriate dimensions determines an
\tilde{I}
up to sign.

Denote the subgroups of elements of
\tilde{G}
that, respectively, commute with
\tilde{I}
,
\tilde{\mathbf{J}}
, and
\tilde{I}\tilde{\mathbf{J}}
by

\displaystyle\tilde{G}^{\shortparallel}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\tilde{G}(\tilde{I})

\displaystyle:=

\displaystyle\tilde{G}^{\operatorname{Ad}(\tilde{I})}

\displaystyle\leq

\displaystyle\tilde{G}

\displaystyle\tilde{K}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\tilde{G}(\tilde{\mathbf{J}})

\displaystyle:=

\displaystyle\tilde{G}^{\operatorname{Ad}(\tilde{\mathbf{J}})}

\displaystyle\leq

\displaystyle\tilde{G}

\displaystyle\tilde{G}(\tilde{I}\tilde{\mathbf{J}})

\displaystyle:=

\displaystyle\tilde{G}^{\operatorname{Ad}(\tilde{I}\tilde{\mathbf{J}})}

\displaystyle\leq

\displaystyle\tilde{G}.

Let

\tilde{K}^{\shortparallel}=\tilde{G}(\tilde{I},\tilde{\mathbf{J}}):=\tilde{G}(\tilde{I})\cap\tilde{G}(\tilde{\mathbf{J}})=\tilde{G}(\tilde{I})\cap\tilde{G}(\tilde{I}\tilde{\mathbf{J}})=\tilde{G}(\tilde{\mathbf{J}})\cap\tilde{G}(\tilde{I}\tilde{\mathbf{J}}).

Let
\tilde{\mathfrak{g}}
be the Lie algebra of
\tilde{G}
. Let
\tilde{\mathfrak{k}}
be the
+1
-eigenspace of
\operatorname{Ad}(\tilde{\mathbf{J}})
and
\tilde{\mathfrak{p}}
be the
-1
-eigenspace of
\operatorname{Ad}(\tilde{\mathbf{J}})
in
\tilde{\mathfrak{g}}
. Let
\tilde{\mathfrak{g}}^{\shortparallel}
be the
+1
-eigenspace, and
\tilde{\mathfrak{g}}^{\times}
be the
-1
-eigenspace of
\operatorname{Ad}(\tilde{I})
in
\tilde{\mathfrak{g}}
. Since
\operatorname{Ad}(\tilde{I})
commutes with
\operatorname{Ad}(\tilde{\mathbf{J}})
,
\tilde{\mathfrak{g}}
has two splittings

\tilde{\mathfrak{g}}=\tilde{\mathfrak{g}}^{\shortparallel}\oplus\tilde{\mathfrak{g}}^{\times}=\tilde{\mathfrak{k}}\oplus\tilde{\mathfrak{p}},

orthogonal with respect to the Killing form, such that

\tilde{\mathfrak{g}}=(\tilde{\mathfrak{g}}^{\shortparallel}\cap\tilde{\mathfrak{k}})\oplus(\tilde{\mathfrak{g}}^{\times}\cap\tilde{\mathfrak{k}})\oplus(\tilde{\mathfrak{g}}^{\shortparallel}\cap\tilde{\mathfrak{p}})\oplus(\tilde{\mathfrak{g}}^{\times}\cap\tilde{\mathfrak{p}})

is also an orthogonal splitting. Denote
\tilde{\mathfrak{k}}^{\shortparallel}:=\tilde{\mathfrak{g}}^{\shortparallel}\cap\tilde{\mathfrak{k}}
,
\mathfrak{k}^{\times}:=\tilde{\mathfrak{g}}^{\times}\cap\tilde{\mathfrak{k}}
,
\tilde{\mathfrak{p}}^{\shortparallel}:=\tilde{\mathfrak{g}}^{\shortparallel}\cap\tilde{\mathfrak{p}}
,
\tilde{\mathfrak{p}}^{\times}:=\tilde{\mathfrak{g}}^{\times}\cap\tilde{\mathfrak{p}}
.

From the descriptions as eigenspaces, we have

\displaystyle\subseteq\tilde{\mathfrak{k}},

\displaystyle[\tilde{\mathfrak{k}},\tilde{\mathfrak{p}}]

\displaystyle\subseteq\tilde{\mathfrak{p}},

\displaystyle[\tilde{\mathfrak{p}},\tilde{\mathfrak{p}}]

\displaystyle\subseteq\tilde{\mathfrak{k}}

(42)

\displaystyle[\tilde{\mathfrak{g}}^{\shortparallel},\tilde{\mathfrak{g}}^{\shortparallel}]

\displaystyle\subseteq\tilde{\mathfrak{g}}^{\shortparallel},

\displaystyle[\tilde{\mathfrak{g}}^{\shortparallel},\tilde{\mathfrak{g}}^{\times}]

\displaystyle\subseteq\tilde{\mathfrak{g}}^{\times},

\displaystyle[\tilde{\mathfrak{g}}^{\times},\tilde{\mathfrak{g}}^{\times}]

\displaystyle\subseteq\tilde{\mathfrak{g}}^{\shortparallel}.

From these conditions we obtain

[\tilde{\mathfrak{k}}^{\shortparallel},\tilde{\mathfrak{k}}^{\shortparallel}]\subseteq\tilde{\mathfrak{k}}^{\shortparallel},\quad[\tilde{\mathfrak{k}}^{\times},\tilde{\mathfrak{k}}^{\times}]\subseteq\tilde{\mathfrak{k}}^{\shortparallel},\quad[\tilde{\mathfrak{p}}^{\shortparallel},\tilde{\mathfrak{p}}^{\shortparallel}]\subseteq\tilde{\mathfrak{k}}^{\shortparallel},\quad[\tilde{\mathfrak{p}}^{\times},\tilde{\mathfrak{p}}^{\times}]\subseteq\tilde{\mathfrak{k}}^{\shortparallel},

(43)

[\tilde{\mathfrak{k}}^{\shortparallel},\tilde{\mathfrak{k}}^{\times}]\subseteq\tilde{\mathfrak{k}}^{\times},\quad[\tilde{\mathfrak{k}}^{\shortparallel},\tilde{\mathfrak{p}}^{\shortparallel}]\subseteq\tilde{\mathfrak{p}}^{\shortparallel},\quad[\tilde{\mathfrak{k}}^{\shortparallel},\tilde{\mathfrak{p}}^{\times}]\subseteq\tilde{\mathfrak{p}}^{\times}.

(44)

In particular
\tilde{\mathfrak{k}}^{\shortparallel}
,
\tilde{\mathfrak{g}}^{\shortparallel}=\tilde{\mathfrak{k}}^{\shortparallel}\oplus\tilde{\mathfrak{p}}^{\shortparallel}
,
\tilde{\mathfrak{k}}=\tilde{\mathfrak{k}}^{\shortparallel}\oplus\tilde{\mathfrak{k}}^{\times}
, and
\tilde{\mathfrak{k}}^{\shortparallel}\oplus\tilde{\mathfrak{p}}^{\times}
are Lie subalgebras of
\tilde{\mathfrak{g}}
, and by (
44
),
\tilde{K}^{\shortparallel}
acts on
\tilde{\mathfrak{p}}
,
\tilde{\mathfrak{p}}^{\shortparallel}
,
\tilde{\mathfrak{p}}^{\times}
,
\tilde{\mathfrak{p}}/\tilde{\mathfrak{p}}^{\shortparallel}
, and
\tilde{\mathfrak{p}}/\tilde{\mathfrak{p}}^{\times}
by
\operatorname{Ad}
. Moreover,
since
\tilde{I}
commutes with
\tilde{\mathbf{J}}
,
\frac{1}{2}\operatorname{ad}(\tilde{\mathbf{J}})
is a linear complex structure (
36
) on
\tilde{\mathfrak{p}}^{\shortparallel}
,
\tilde{\mathfrak{p}}^{\times}
,
\tilde{\mathfrak{p}}
,
\tilde{\mathfrak{p}}/\tilde{\mathfrak{p}}^{\shortparallel}
, and
\tilde{\mathfrak{p}}/\tilde{\mathfrak{p}}^{\times}
compatible with the restrictions and reductions of the Killing form of
\tilde{\mathfrak{g}}
.

By the correspondence between Lie groups and Lie algebras,

\displaystyle\tilde{\mathfrak{g}}^{\shortparallel}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\operatorname{Lie}\tilde{G}(\tilde{I})

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\tilde{\mathfrak{k}}^{\shortparallel}\oplus\tilde{\mathfrak{p}}^{\shortparallel}

\displaystyle\tilde{\mathfrak{k}}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\operatorname{Lie}\tilde{G}(\tilde{\mathbf{J}})

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\tilde{\mathfrak{k}}^{\shortparallel}\oplus\tilde{\mathfrak{k}}^{\times}

\displaystyle\operatorname{Lie}\tilde{G}(\tilde{I}\tilde{\mathbf{J}})

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\tilde{\mathfrak{k}}^{\shortparallel}\oplus\tilde{\mathfrak{p}}^{\times}

\displaystyle\operatorname{Lie}\tilde{G}(\tilde{I},\tilde{\mathbf{J}})

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\tilde{\mathfrak{k}}^{\shortparallel}.

By Theorem
2.3
there exists a Darboux basis
\{\tilde{\mathbf{e}},\tilde{\mathbf{J}}\tilde{\mathbf{e}}\}
of
\tilde{W}
such that
\tilde{I}
and
\tilde{\mathbf{J}}
are expressed in this basis as

(\tilde{I})_{\{\tilde{\mathbf{e}},\tilde{\mathbf{J}}\tilde{\mathbf{e}}\}}=\begin{pmatrix}1_{n_{+}}&0&0&0\\
0&-1_{n_{-}}&0&0\\
0&0&1_{n_{+}}&0\\
0&0&0&-1_{n_{-}}\end{pmatrix},\quad(\tilde{\mathbf{J}})_{\{\tilde{\mathbf{e}},\tilde{\mathbf{J}}\tilde{\mathbf{e}}\}}=\begin{pmatrix}0&-1_{n-n_{0}}\\
1_{n-n_{0}}&0\end{pmatrix}.

(45)

Then we can write the elements of
\tilde{\mathfrak{g}}
in this basis as block matrices

\begin{pmatrix}\mathbf{a}_{++}&\mathbf{a}_{+-}&\mathbf{b}_{++}&\mathbf{b}_{+-}\\
\mathbf{a}_{-+}&\mathbf{a}_{--}&\mathbf{b}_{-+}&\mathbf{b}_{--}\\
\mathbf{c}_{++}&\mathbf{c}_{+-}&\mathbf{d}_{++}&\mathbf{d}_{+-}\\
\mathbf{c}_{-+}&\mathbf{c}_{--}&\mathbf{d}_{-+}&\mathbf{d}_{--}\end{pmatrix}

such that

\mathbf{a}_{\pm\pm}=-\mathbf{d}_{\pm\pm}^{t}\in\operatorname{Mat}_{n_{\pm}\times n_{\pm}}(\mathbb{R}),\quad\mathbf{b}_{\pm\pm},\mathbf{c}_{\pm,\pm}\in\operatorname{Mat}_{n_{\pm}\times n_{\pm}}(\mathbb{R})^{t},

(46)

\mathbf{a}_{-+}=-\mathbf{d}_{+-}^{t},\mathbf{a}_{+-}=-\mathbf{d}_{-+}^{t},\mathbf{b}_{-+}=\mathbf{b}_{+-}^{t},\mathbf{c}_{-+}=\mathbf{c}_{+-}^{t},\in\operatorname{Mat}_{n_{-}\times n_{+}}(\mathbb{R}).

(47)

In matrix form the eigenspaces are

\displaystyle\tilde{\mathfrak{k}}^{\shortparallel}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\left\{\begin{pmatrix}\mathbf{a}_{++}&0&\mathbf{b}_{++}&0\\
0&\mathbf{a}_{--}&0&\mathbf{b}_{--}\\
-\mathbf{b}_{++}&0&\mathbf{a}_{++}&0\\
\ 0&-\mathbf{b}_{--}&0&\mathbf{a}_{--}\end{pmatrix}:\begin{aligned} \mathbf{a}_{\pm\pm}&\in\operatorname{Mat}_{n_{\pm}\times n_{\pm}}(\mathbb{R})^{-t}\\
\mathbf{b}_{\pm\pm}&\in\operatorname{Mat}_{n_{\pm}\times n_{\pm}}(\mathbb{R})^{t}\end{aligned}\right\}

(48)

\displaystyle\tilde{\mathfrak{p}}^{\shortparallel}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\left\{\begin{pmatrix}\mathbf{a}_{++}&0&\mathbf{b}_{++}&0\\
0&\mathbf{a}_{--}&0&\mathbf{b}_{--}\\
\mathbf{b}_{++}&0&-\mathbf{a}_{++}&0\\
0&\mathbf{b}_{--}&0&-\mathbf{a}_{--}\end{pmatrix}:\begin{aligned} \mathbf{a}_{\pm\pm}&\in\operatorname{Mat}_{n_{\pm}\times n_{\pm}}(\mathbb{R})^{t}\\
\mathbf{b}_{\pm\pm}&\in\operatorname{Mat}_{n_{\pm}\times n_{\pm}}(\mathbb{R})^{t}\end{aligned}\right\}

(49)

\displaystyle\tilde{\mathfrak{k}}^{\times}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\left\{\begin{pmatrix}0&-\mathbf{a}_{-+}^{t}&0&\mathbf{b}_{-+}^{t}\\
\mathbf{a}_{-+}&0&\mathbf{b}_{-+}&0\\
0&-\mathbf{b}_{-+}^{t}&0&-\mathbf{a}_{-+}^{t}\\
-\mathbf{b}_{-+}&0&\mathbf{a}_{-+}&0\end{pmatrix}:\mathbf{a}_{-+},\mathbf{b}_{-+}\in\operatorname{Mat}_{n_{-}\times n_{+}}(\mathbb{R})\right\}

(50)

\displaystyle\tilde{\mathfrak{p}}^{\times}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\left\{\begin{pmatrix}0&\mathbf{a}_{-+}^{t}&0&\mathbf{b}_{-+}^{t}\\
\mathbf{a}_{-+}&0&\mathbf{b}_{-+}&0\\
0&\mathbf{b}_{-+}^{t}&0&-\mathbf{a}_{-+}^{t}\\
\mathbf{b}_{-+}&0&-\mathbf{a}_{-+}&0\end{pmatrix}:\mathbf{a}_{-+},\mathbf{b}_{-+}\in\operatorname{Mat}_{n_{-}\times n_{+}}(\mathbb{R})\right\}.

(51)

Conditions (
46
), (
47
) also imply

\begin{pmatrix}\mathbf{a}_{\pm\pm}&\mathbf{b}_{\pm\pm}\\
\mathbf{c}_{\pm\pm}&\mathbf{d}_{\pm\pm}\end{pmatrix}\in\mathfrak{sp}(2n_{\pm};\mathbb{R})

and if
\mathbf{a}_{\pm\pm}\in\operatorname{Mat}_{n_{\pm}\times n_{\pm}}(\mathbb{R})^{-t}
then
\mathbf{a}_{\pm\pm}+i\mathbf{b}_{\pm\pm}\in\mathfrak{u}(n_{\pm})
,

\begin{pmatrix}\mathbf{a}_{++}+i\mathbf{b}_{++}&-\mathbf{a}_{-+}^{t}+i\mathbf{b}_{-+}^{t}\\
\mathbf{a}_{-+}+i\mathbf{b}_{-+}&\mathbf{a}_{--}+i\mathbf{b}_{--}\end{pmatrix}\in\mathfrak{u}(n),\quad\begin{pmatrix}\mathbf{a}_{++}+i\mathbf{b}_{++}&\mathbf{a}_{-+}^{t}+i\mathbf{b}_{-+}^{t}\\
\mathbf{a}_{-+}-i\mathbf{b}_{-+}&\mathbf{a}_{--}-i\mathbf{b}_{--}\end{pmatrix}\in\mathfrak{u}(n_{+},n_{-}).

So we have isomorphisms of vector spaces

\displaystyle\tilde{\mathfrak{k}}^{\shortparallel}\oplus\tilde{\mathfrak{p}}^{\shortparallel}

\displaystyle\cong

\displaystyle\mathfrak{sp}(2n_{+};\mathbb{R})\times\mathfrak{sp}(2n_{-};\mathbb{R})

\displaystyle\tilde{\mathfrak{k}}^{\shortparallel}\oplus\tilde{\mathfrak{k}}^{\times}

\displaystyle\cong

\displaystyle\mathfrak{u}(n)

\displaystyle\tilde{\mathfrak{k}}^{\shortparallel}\oplus\tilde{\mathfrak{p}}^{\times}

\displaystyle\cong

\displaystyle\mathfrak{u}(n_{+},n_{-})

\displaystyle\tilde{\mathfrak{k}}^{\shortparallel}

\displaystyle\cong

\displaystyle\mathfrak{u}(n_{+})\times\mathfrak{u}(n_{-})

that are also isomorphism of Lie algebras. In the matrix forms, we can also verify ((
20
), (
21
))

\displaystyle\tilde{G}(\tilde{I})

\displaystyle\cong

\displaystyle\operatorname{Sp}(2n_{+};\mathbb{R})\times\operatorname{Sp}(2n_{-};\mathbb{R})

\displaystyle\tilde{G}(\tilde{\mathbf{J}})

\displaystyle\cong

\displaystyle\operatorname{U}(n_{+}+n_{-})

\displaystyle\tilde{G}(\tilde{I}\tilde{\mathbf{J}})

\displaystyle\cong

\displaystyle\operatorname{U}(n_{+},n_{-})

\displaystyle\tilde{G}(\tilde{I},\tilde{\mathbf{J}})

\displaystyle\cong

\displaystyle\operatorname{U}(n_{+})\times\operatorname{U}(n_{-}).

Definition 3.15

(Compatible triples)
.

Suppose
W_{0}\in\operatorname{Gr}(n_{0},0,n-n_{0};V)
, and suppose
\tilde{I}
,
\tilde{\mathbf{J}}
are as above.

1.

If
\mathbf{F}^{\oplus}\in\alpha^{-1}(W_{0})
, a triple
(\tilde{I},\tilde{\mathbf{J}},\mathbf{F}^{\oplus})
is a
compatible triple
if

\tilde{G}(\tilde{I},\tilde{\mathbf{J}})=\operatorname{Sp}_{\mathbf{F}^{\oplus}/\mathbf{F}_{0}}(\tilde{W})\cap\tilde{G}(\tilde{\mathbf{J}}).

2.

If
\mathbf{F}\in(\operatorname{Re}\cdot_{0})^{-1}(W_{0})
, a triple
(\tilde{I},\tilde{\mathbf{J}},\mathbf{F})
is a
compatible triple
if

\tilde{G}(\tilde{I},\tilde{\mathbf{J}})=\operatorname{Sp}_{\mathbf{F}/\mathbf{F}_{0}}(\tilde{W})\cap\tilde{G}(\tilde{\mathbf{J}}).

3.

If
W^{\prime}\in(\cdot\cap\cdot^{\omega})^{-1}(W_{0})
, a triple
(\tilde{I},\tilde{\mathbf{J}},W^{\prime})
is a
compatible triple
if

\tilde{G}(\tilde{I},\tilde{\mathbf{J}})=\operatorname{Sp}_{W^{\prime}/W_{0}}(\tilde{W})\cap\tilde{G}(\mathbf{J}).

By Theorems
2.2
,
2.10
, and (
45
), for every pair
(\tilde{I},\tilde{\mathbf{J}})
, there exists
\mathbf{F}^{\oplus}\in\alpha^{-1}(W_{0})
,
\mathbf{F}\in(\operatorname{Re}\cdot_{0})^{-1}(W_{0})
,
W\in(\cdot\cap\cdot^{\omega})^{-1}(W_{0})
such that
(\tilde{I},\tilde{\mathbf{J}},\mathbf{F}^{\oplus})
,
(\tilde{I},\tilde{\mathbf{J}},\mathbf{F})
,
(\tilde{I},\tilde{\mathbf{J}},W^{\prime})
are compatible triples. Conversely, for every
\mathbf{F}^{\oplus}\in\alpha^{-1}(W_{0})
,
\mathbf{F}\in(\operatorname{Re}\cdot_{0})^{-1}(W_{0})
,
W^{\prime}\in(\cdot\cap\cdot^{\omega})^{-1}(W_{0})
, there exist
(\tilde{I},\tilde{\mathbf{J}})
such that
(\tilde{I},\tilde{\mathbf{J}},\mathbf{F}^{\oplus})
,
(\tilde{I},\tilde{\mathbf{J}},\mathbf{F})
,
(\tilde{I},\tilde{\mathbf{J}},W^{\prime})
are compatible triples.

3.4
Fibers of
\operatorname{Re}\cdot_{\geq}0
and
\varphi
as symmetric spaces

We have the tower of real Lie groups

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\tilde{G}=\operatorname{Sp}(\tilde{W})}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\tilde{G}^{\shortparallel}=\tilde{G}(\tilde{I})}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\tilde{K}=\tilde{G}(\tilde{\mathbf{J}})}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\tilde{G}(\tilde{I}\tilde{\mathbf{J}})}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\tilde{K}^{\shortparallel}=\tilde{G}(\tilde{I},\tilde{\mathbf{J}}).}

Then from Lemma

and Lemma

.3.c we obtain the following fiber bundles:

\displaystyle\tilde{G}(\tilde{I})/\tilde{G}(\tilde{I},\tilde{\mathbf{J}})\hookrightarrow

\displaystyle\twoheadrightarrow\tilde{G}/\tilde{G}(\tilde{I})

\displaystyle\tilde{G}/\tilde{G}(\tilde{I},\tilde{\mathbf{J}})

\displaystyle\twoheadrightarrow\tilde{G}/\tilde{G}(\tilde{\mathbf{J}})

(54)

\displaystyle\tilde{G}(\tilde{I}\tilde{\mathbf{J}})/\tilde{G}(\tilde{I},\tilde{\mathbf{J}})\hookrightarrow

\displaystyle\tilde{G}/\tilde{G}(\tilde{I},\tilde{\mathbf{J}})

\displaystyle\twoheadrightarrow\tilde{G}/\tilde{G}(\tilde{I}\tilde{\mathbf{J}}).

Now choose an

The fiber bundle in (

) can be identified with the the fiber bundle
\beta
of the symplectic twistor Grassmannian in Proposition 17 of
[
